Explicit error bounds with commutator scaling for time-dependent product and multi-product formulas
Kaoru Mizuta, Tatsuhiko N. Ikeda, Keisuke Fujii

Abstract
Product formula (PF), which approximates the time evolution under a many-body Hamiltonian by the product of local time-evolution operators, is one of the central approaches for simulating quantum dynamics by quantum computers. It has been of great interest whether the errors of PFs can be expressed by commutators among local terms (called commutator scaling ), since it brings the substantial suppression of the computational cost in the system size. Although recent studies have revealed the error bounds with commutator scaling for generic time-independent systems, those for time-dependent Hamiltonians remain to be a difficult problem except for low-order PFs. In this paper, we derive an error bound of generic PFs for smooth time-dependent Hamiltonians, which is expressed explicitly by nested commutators and their time derivatives. It indicates the substantial error suppression in the system size for generic local Hamiltonians, thereby giving a much better estimate of gate counts. Our derivation employs Floquet theory originally developed for time-periodic systems, with which we analyze the time-dependent PF errors by mapping them to those for time-independent PFs defined on an infinite-dimensional space. This approach is also applicable to a family of time-dependent PFs, such as the one for non-unitary dynamics. In particular, we clarify the error bound of a time-dependent multi-product formula, with which we can achieve exponentially better scaling with respect to the target accuracy than the PF. Our results will shed light on applications of near-term and future quantum computers, ranging from quantum simulation of nonequilibrium materials to faster algorithms exploiting time-dependent Hamiltonians such as adiabatic state preparation.



I
Introduction

Simulating time evolution of quantum many-body systems, called Hamiltonian simulation, is one of the most promising tasks of quantum computers with the essential quantum speedup.
Owing to its broad application to physics and chemistry, various quantum algorithms such as Trotterization
[
34
]
and quantum singular value transformation (QSVT)
[
35
,
36
,
16
]
have been vigorously developed.
Trotterization has been one of the central approaches for a long time, which constructs an approximate time-evolution operator under a Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}
by the product of those under simple Hamiltonians
\{H_{\gamma}\}
based on the product formulas (PFs)
[
34
]
.
It guarantees accuracy and also provides a simple construction of quantum circuits by local quantum gates without using any ancilla qubits, making it being a promising approach both for near-term and long-term quantum computers.

The error of the PFs from the exact time-evolution operator is the most significant issue that determines the computational cost of Hamiltonian simulation.
For time-independent Hamiltonians, there have been much development in understanding the error bounds of the PFs for generic orders.
Firstly, the
p
-th order PF has been proven to have an error bound
\order{(\sum_\gamma\norm{H_\gamma} t)^{p+1}}
, which is called the
1
-norm scaling
[
6
,
47
,
21
]
.
However, there exists a better bound having so-called the commutator scaling, which states that the coefficient of the
p
-th order PF error
\order{t^{p+1}}
is proportional to the summation of
(p+1)
-fold commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}\}
.
The commutator scaling substantially suppresses the error and thereby the gate counts in terms of the system size for generic local Hamiltonians compared to the
1
-norm scaling.
It has been well known for the low-order PFs from the context of Lie algebra
[
54
]
, and a series of studies have revealed it also for higher-orders
[
53
,
13
]
.
Importantly, Ref.
[
12
]
has derived the error bound of generic time-independent PFs explicitly expressed by the commutators among the terms
\{H_{\gamma}\}
, which is applicable to various local Hamiltonians having finite- or long-ranged interactions.
Such commutator scaling contributes to better gate count estimates for simulating a variety of spin or electronic systems
[
61
,
5
,
49
,
11
]
.

On the other hand, it is contrasting for time-dependent Hamiltonians.
While the commutator-scaling error has been understood for low order time-dependent PF errors as well
[
25
,
46
,
48
,
61
,
3
,
26
]
, the error bounds for generic time-dependent PFs remain to be a difficult problem.
Ref.
[
62
]
shows an error bound for smooth time-dependent Hamiltonians including higher orders, which corresponds to the error with the
1
-norm scaling.
Ref.
[
13
]
shows a bound involving commutators in a similar manner to time-independent cases, but the commutators include a fraction of the time-dependent PFs, which are difficult to compute except for finite-ranged Hamiltonians.

In this paper, we derive the explicit error bound of generic time-dependent PFs.
We consider generic smooth time-dependent Hamiltonians in the form of
H(t)=\sum_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}(t)
.
We show that the error bound for the
p
-th order, which amounts to
\order{t^{p+1}}
, have a coefficient expressed by commutators among the set of terms
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their derivatives in time
\{H_{\gamma}^{(q)}(t)\}
for
q=1,\cdots,p
.
This scaling hosts the substantial suppression with respect to the system size for local Hamiltonians with finite-, short-, and long-ranged interactions like time-independent cases.
In contrast to previous studies, our error bound simultaneously achieves the commutator scaling for generic orders and its applicability to various time-dependent local Hamiltonians.
We also estimate the gate count for Hamiltonian simulations based on the time-dependent PFs.
They are substantially improved in the system size for generic local finite-, short-, and long-ranged Hamiltonians, which becomes almost as small as time-independent cases.

Our strategy makes use of Floquet theory, originally developed for time-periodic Hamiltonians, as a mathematical tool to analyze general smooth time-dependent Hamiltonians.
By mapping a generic time-dependent Hamiltonian to a time-independent one acting on an infinite-dimensional extended space
[
45
]
, we can derive the error bound in three steps.
First, based on the Floquet mapping, we prove the existence of a time-independent PF on an infinite-dimensional space, which can reproduce a generic time-dependent PF.
Next, using a technique developed for time-independent cases
[
12
]
, we obtain the error representation for the time-independent PF on the infinite-dimensional space.
Finally, we reduce this error to the one for the time-dependent PF in the original space.
By carefully showing the convergence of the error by the inverse Floquet mapping, we arrive at the error bound explicitly represented by nested commutators and their time derivatives.

Our formalism also applies to various families of time-dependent PFs, such as the time-dependent multi-product formula (MPF) and the time-dependent PF for non-unitary dynamics.
In particular, MPF is a promising approach that can exponentially improve the gate counts with respect to the allowable error
\varepsilon

[
14
,
37
]
.
While the commutator scaling of MPF error has recently been proven for time-independent Hamiltonians
[
64
,
1
,
43
]
, that for time-dependent ones has been an open problem
[
60
]
.
Our approach reveals the commutator-scaling-type error of the time-dependent MPFs, resulting in their efficiency with respect to the system size and the accuracy.

Our error bound will directly contribute to offering better estimations of costs for various tasks of quantum computation, such as simulation of nonequilibrium materials, adiabatic state preparation
[
63
,
4
,
2
]
, and even the exploration of time-independent systems using the interaction picture
[
39
,
9
,
51
]
.
We also expect that our derivation based on Floquet theory enables us to explore various families of time-dependent PFs.
Our results will shed light on the application of near-term and future fault-tolerant quantum computers toward simulation of quantum physics and chemistry.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
In Section
II
, we briefly review time-independent PFs and their commutator-scaling errors.
We also provide the construction of time-dependent PFs.
In Section
III
, we summarize our main results on the error bound of the time-dependent PFs.
Its derivation ranges from Section
IV
to Section
V
.
In Section
VI
, we derive the gate count estimates for generic local Hamiltonians, in which we confirm the efficiency brought by the commutator scaling for time-dependent cases.
Section
VII
extends our result to time-independent MPFs.
We conclude our paper in Section
VIII
.
We provide Appendices that include known results for completeness and detailed proofs of our results.

II
Preliminary

In this section, we introduce some notations used in this paper (Section
II.1
), and briefly review the errors of the time-independent and time-dependent PFs (Sections
II.2
) and
II.3
.

II.1
Notation

•

Norm:
\norm{A}
for an operator
A
denotes its operator norm.

\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}
for a vector
\vec{c}=(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots)
represents its
1
-norm
\sum_{j}|c_{j}|
.

•

Product of operators: For a series of operators
U_{1},U_{2},\cdots,U_{K}
, we define their product with arrows by

\qquad\prod_{k=1}^{K}U_{k}=U_{K}\cdots U_{1},\quad\prod_{k=K}^{1}U_{k}=U_{1}\cdots U_{K}.

(1)

•

Tuple of indices: An index
v\gamma
denotes a pair of indices
(v,\gamma)
.
When
v
and
\gamma
are respectively chosen from
\{1,2,\cdots,V\}
and
\{1,2,\cdots,\Gamma\}
, we align the indices in the order,
11,12,\cdots,1\Gamma,21,22,\cdots,V\Gamma
.
The notation
v\gamma\prec v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}
means that
v\gamma
precedes
v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}
in this order, and the symbols
\preceq,\succ,\succeq
are defined similarly.
The index
v(\gamma+1)
in the case of
\gamma=\Gamma+1
means
(v+1)1
.
The index
v(\gamma-1)
for
\gamma=1
means
(v-1)\Gamma
as well.

•

Commutators: For operators
A
and
B
, we use
[A,B]
or
\mathrm{ad}_{A}B
to represent their commutator
AB-BA
.
We also note that
e^{\mathrm{ad}_{A}}B=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(\mathrm{ad}_{A})^{n}B=e^{A}Be^{-A}
holds if
(\mathrm{ad}_{A})^{n}B
is bounded for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Derivative: For a time-dependent operator
A(t)
, its derivative
A^{(p)}(t)
means
\derivative[p]{t}A(t)
.

•

Time evolution: For a time-dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)
, its time-evolution operator from
t_{0}
to
t+t_{0}
is

\quad U(t+t_{0},t_{0})=\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t+t_{0}}\differential t^{\prime}H(t^{\prime})\right).

(2)

The symbol
\mathcal{T}
denotes a time-ordered product.

•

Landau symbol: The symbols
\order{\cdot}
,
o(\cdot)
, and
\Omega(\cdot)
denote the Landau symbols defined by

\displaystyle\order{f(x)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{g(x)\,|\,\lim_{x\to\text{$0$ or $\infty$}}\left|\frac{g(x)}{f(x)}\right|<\infty\right\},

(3)

\displaystyle o(f(x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{g(x)\,|\,\lim_{x\to\text{$0$ or $\infty$}}\left|\frac{g(x)}{f(x)}\right|=0\right\},

(4)

\displaystyle\Omega(f(x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{g(x)\,|\,\lim_{x\to\text{$0$ or $\infty$}}\left|\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\right|<\infty\right\}.

(5)

The symbols
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\cdot\right)
,
\tilde{o}(\cdot)
, and
\tilde{\Omega}(\cdot)
allow poly-logarithmic corrections in the variables.

II.2
Time-independent product formula

We briefly review the PF for time-independent systems and its commutator scaling.
Consider a time-independent Hamiltonian
H
given by

H=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}.

(6)

The partition
\gamma
is determined so that the time-evolution operators
\{e^{-iH_{\gamma}\tau}\}
are easily implemented by local quantum gates for any
\tau
.
The aim of the PF is to approximate
e^{-iHt}
under sufficiently small time
t
by the product of
\{e^{-iH_{\gamma}\tau}\}
.
To be precise, the
p
th-order PF is defined by

T(t)=\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}\alpha_{v\gamma}t},

(7)

satisfying the order condition
T(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
in
t\to 0
.
The symbol
\pi_{v}
denotes the permutation of the indices
\{1,2,\cdots,\Gamma\}
depending on the stage
v\in\{1,2,\cdots,V_{p}\}
.
The number of stages
V_{p}
is an
\order{1}
constant under
p\in\order{1}
, while it is usually large exponentially in
p
.

The most famous example is so-called Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula
[
56
]
.
The first- and second-order formulas are given by

T_{1}(t)=\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}e^{-iH_{\gamma}t},\quad T_{2}(t)=T_{1}(-t/2)^{\dagger}T_{1}(t/2).

(8)

Higher-order formulas
T_{2q}(t)
for
q\in\mathbb{N}
are obtained by the recursive relation,

T_{2q}(t)=T_{2q-2}(a_{q}t)^{2}T_{2q-2}(b_{q}t)^{\dagger}T_{2q-2}(a_{q}t)^{2}.

(9)

The positive factors
a_{q}
and
b_{q}
are respectively defined by
a_{q}=(4-4^{1/(2q-1)})^{-1}
and
b_{q}=4a_{q}-1
.
We have
V_{p}=1
(for
p=1
) and
V_{p}=2\cdot 5^{q-1}
(for
p=2q
).
The permutation
\pi_{v}
is designated by

\pi_{v}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\gamma&(\text{$v$: odd}),\\
\Gamma+1-\gamma&(\text{$v$: even}).\end{cases}

(10)

Throughout this work,
T(t)
without a subscript represents an arbitrary time-independent PF.
On the other hand,
T_{p}(t)
having a subscript
p
denotes the
p
th-order Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula.

The error of the PF,
\norm{e^{-iHt}-T(t)}
, is of great interest since it determines the gate counts for Hamiltonian simulation.
The prototypical form of the PF error is so-called the
1
-norm scaling
[
6
,
47
,
21
]
, expressed by

\norm{e^{-iHt}-T(t)}\in\order{\left( \sum_\gamma\norm{H_\gamma} t\right)^{p+1}}.

(11)

However, this formula overestimates the error since it becomes nonzero even when
\{H_{\gamma}\}
mutually commutes.
There exists a better bound resolving such a problem, given by

\norm{e^{-iHt}-T(t)}\in\order{\alpha_{\mr{com},p} t^{p+1}}.

(12)

It is called commutator scaling since the factor
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}
is given by

\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}=\sum_{\gamma_{0},\cdots,\gamma_{p}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{[H_{\gamma_p},[H_{\gamma_{p-1}},\cdots,[H_{\gamma_1},H_{\gamma_0}]]]}.

(13)

While this better bound has been well known for low order cases over the past decades
[
54
]
, the one for generic time-independent formulas has been proven recently
[
53
,
13
,
12
]
.

The commutator scaling contributes to reducing the computational cost.
When considering large evolution time
t
, the query complexity
r
in the PF is determined so that the desirable error
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
can be achieved by

\norm{e^{-iHt}-\{T(t/r)\}^r}\leq\varepsilon.

(14)

The number
r
is called the Trotter number.
The commutator scaling error by Eq. (
12
) brings substantially better dependency in the system size than the one based on the
1
-norm scaling Eq. (
11
) and those of post-Trotter algorithms like LCU and QSVT for various local Hamiltonians
[
61
,
5
,
49
,
11
]
.
The commutator scaling is essential for PFs, but it has not been fully understood in time-dependent cases.

II.3
Time-dependent product formula

In this section, we introduce time-dependent PFs.
Let us consider a time-dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)
, decomposed by

H(t)=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}(t),

(15)

and construct the approximation of the time-evolution operator
U(t+t_{0},t_{0})
.
We set
t_{0}=0
without loss of generality since we can reproduce the results for
t_{0}\neq 0
by shifting the time by
t_{0}
.
In contrast to time-independent systems, there are various candidates for time-dependent PFs, originating from the existence of the time-ordered product.
Throughout this paper, we focus on the following two formulations.

Standard PFs.—

The standard PF is composed of time-evolution operators under instantaneous Hamiltonians as

\bar{S}(t,0)=\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}t)\alpha_{v\gamma}t},

(16)

where the coefficients
\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
are determined by the order condition
\bar{S}(t,0)=U(t,0)+\order{t^{p+1}}

[
56
]
.
Each time evolution under the instantaneous Hamiltonian
H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}t)
is implemented by local gates as well as time-independent cases.
As the simplest construction, the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formulas are given by

\bar{S}_{1}(t,0)=\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}e^{-iH_{\gamma}(0)t},\quad\bar{S}_{2}(t,0)=\bar{S}_{1}(t/2,t)^{\dagger}\bar{S}_{1}(t/2,0).

(17)

The higher-order formulas are obtained by the recursive relation,

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{2q}(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{2q-2}(t,t-a_{q}t)\bar{S}_{2q-2}(t-a_{q}t,t-2a_{q}t)

(18)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bar{S}_{2q-2}(2a_{q}t,t-2a_{q}t)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\bar{S}_{2q-2}(2a_{q}t,a_{q}t)\bar{S}_{2q-2}(a_{q}t,0),

with
a_{q}=[4-4^{1/(2q-1)}]^{-1}
.
The error bound of the standard PFs has been vigorously investigated in the past decade, while the one for low-orders are well-established
[
56
,
57
]
.
For generic orders
p\in\mathbb{N}
, it is shown to have the error bound,

\displaystyle\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)}\in\order{\left\{\max_{\tau\in[0,t]} \left[ \alpha_{\text{1-norm},p}(\tau)\right] t\right\}^{p+1}},

(19)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\text{1-norm},p}(\tau)=\sum_{\gamma}\max_{q=0,\cdots,p}\left(\norm{\dv[q]{\tau} H_\gamma(\tau)}^{\frac{1}{q+1}}\right),

(20)

which is a counterpart of the
1
-norm scaling by Eq. (
11
)
[
62
]
.
Another study
[
13
]
has revealed the error bound expressed by nested commutators.
While it can provide size-efficient cost for generic local Hamiltonians with finite-range interactions, it includes matrix exponentials which are difficult to be evaluated for Hamiltonians with long-range interactions.

Generalized PFs.—

The other time-dependent PF is called the generalized PF, which is organized by the time-evolution operators,

U_{\gamma}(t+t_{0},t_{0})=\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-i\int_{t_{0}}^{t+t_{0}}\differential t^{\prime}H_{\gamma}(t^{\prime})\right).

(21)

The
p
th-order generalized PF
S(t,0)
is defined by

S(t,0)=\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}U_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}t+\alpha_{v\gamma}t,\beta_{v\gamma}t)

(22)

satisfying the order condition,
S(t,0)=U(t,0)+\order{t^{p+1}}
.
The factors
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
are not necessarily the same as those for the standard PF in Eq. (
16
).
The time-ordered integral in each time evolution
U_{\gamma}(t,0)
can be efficiently computed by classical computers when the partition
\gamma
is properly chosen.
Thus, the generalized PF
S(t,0)
can be implemented by local gates as simply as the standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
.
The Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formulas in this case are given by

S_{1}(t,0)=\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}U_{\gamma}(t,0),\quad S_{2}(t,0)=S_{1}(t/2,t)^{\dagger}S_{1}(t/2,0),

(23)

for the orders
p=1,2
, and the higher-orders are obtained recursively in the same way as Eq. (
18
).
The error bound of the generalized PFs is investigated only for low-orders.
For instance, the first-order Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula
S_{1}(t,0)
has the commutator-scaling error in the form of

\norm{U(t,0)-S_1(t,0)}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\differential t_{2}\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\differential t_{1}\norm{[H_1(t_1),H_2(t_2)]},

(24)

for a Hamiltonian
H(t)=H_{1}(t)+H_{2}(t)
.

Throughout this paper, we make several assumptions on the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
, which do not essentially lose the generality.
First, we assume that the relations

|\alpha_{v\gamma}|\leq 1,\quad\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}:\pi_{v}(\gamma^{\prime})=\gamma}\alpha_{v\gamma^{\prime}}=1

(25)

are satisfied for every
\gamma=1,2,\cdots,\Gamma
.
The former one comes from that each evolution time
|\alpha_{v\gamma}|t
is smaller than
t
.
The latter one means that the total evolution time under
H_{\gamma}(\cdot)
is
t
for every
\gamma
.
In addition, we assume

\begin{cases}0\leq\beta_{v\gamma}\leq 1&\text{for $\bar{S}(t,0)$}\\
0\leq\beta_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}+\alpha_{v\gamma}\leq 1&\text{for $S(t,0)$}\end{cases},

(26)

which means a natural expectation that the time evolution
U(t,0)
can be reproduced by the Hamiltonian
H_{\gamma}(\tau)
during
\tau\in[0,t]
.
Finally, for making the calculation simple, we assume

\beta_{11}=0

(27)

throughout this paper, which means that the approximation begins with the origin of the time.
Although
\beta_{11}
can be nonzero in general, the results for
\beta_{11}\neq 0
are essentially the same as those for
\beta_{11}=0
(See Appendix
B.2
).

As a special case, we also define the PF with the uniform coefficients as follows.

Definition 1
.

(PF with uniform coefficients)

If a standard or generalized PF has the coefficients
\{\beta_{v\gamma}\}
independent of
\gamma
, i.e.,

\beta_{v\gamma}=\beta_{v}\in\mathbb{R},\quad\text{for $v=1,\cdots,V_{p}$},

(28)

it has uniform coefficients.

Since each
\gamma
equally contributes the Hamiltonian, many of the existing PFs such as the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula and the Forest-Ruth-Suzuki formula
[
15
,
55
]
satisfy the uniformity.
Like time-independent cases,
\bar{S}(t,0)
and
S(t,0)
denote arbitrary standard and generalized PFs satisfying the order condition.
On the other hand,
\bar{S}_{p}(t,0)
and
S_{p}(t,0)
mean those with the uniform coefficients, while we often focus on the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formulas as their particular examples without loss of generality.

It is an important but unresolved problem whether they have an error bound expressed by nested commutators, which is applicable to generic orders
q
and generic local Hamiltonians.
It plays a central role for determining whether PFs can simulate time-dependent systems with the cost favorable in the system size like the time-independent cases
[
13
]
.

II.4
Floquet theory

We briefly review Floquet theory and pick up some relation which are frequently used in the proof.
Floquet theory is developed for analyzing time-periodic Hamiltonians in the form of
H(t)=\sum_{m}H_{m}e^{-im\omega t}
.
Each operator
H_{m}
means the Fourier coefficient and is assumed to be
\norm{H_m}\in o(|m|^{-1})
for the absolute and uniform convergence of the Fourier series.
The constant
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the frequency.

In Floquet theory, we consider an infinite-dimensional space (Floquet-Hilbert space), defined by

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{FH}}=l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathcal{H},

(29)

where
l^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
denotes a square-summable space over
\mathbb{Z}
.
A Floquet Hamiltonian
H^{F}
is an operator acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{FH}}
, which is defined by

H^{F}=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{m}\ket{l+m}\bra{l}\otimes H_{m}-l\omega\ket{l}\bra{l}\otimes I\right).

(30)

The ancilla system
\ket{l}
contains the Fourier index
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.
It is often separated into two terms as
H^{F}=H^{\mathrm{Add}}-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
with

H^{\mathrm{Add}}=\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes H_{m},\quad\mathrm{Add}_{m}=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\ket{l+m}\bra{l},

(31)

and the linear-potential term,

H^{\mathrm{LP}}=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}l\omega\ket{l}\bra{l}\otimes I.

(32)

Floquet theory dictates that the time evolution under the time-periodic Hamiltonian
H(t)
can be expressed by the one under the time-independent operator
H^{F}
as

U(t+t_{0},t_{0})=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega(t+t_{0})}\braket{l|e^{-iH^{F}t}|0},

(33)

which is absolutely and uniformly convergent under
\norm{H_m}\in o(|m|^{-1})

[
32
,
40
]
.
Instead of discarding the difficulty by time dependence,
H^{F}
is an unbounded operator on the infinite-dimensional system.

We introduce the translation symmetry in Floquet theory, which will be frequently used in the proof.
We can immediately check the invariance under the shift of the Fourier index,

\displaystyle\braket{l+l^{\prime}|H^{\mathrm{Add}}|l^{\prime}}=\braket{l|H^{\mathrm{Add}}|0},

(34)

\displaystyle\braket{l+l^{\prime}|H^{\mathrm{LP}}|l^{\prime}}=\braket{l|H^{\mathrm{LP}}|0}+l^{\prime}\omega,

(35)

for any
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
from their definitions, Eqs. (
31
) and (
32
).
Using these relations, we also have the symmetry,

\braket{l+l^{\prime}|e^{-iH^{F}t}|l^{\prime}}=e^{il^{\prime}\omega t}\braket{l|e^{-iH^{F}t}|0},

(36)

for any
l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
and any time
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

III
Summary of results

III.1
Setup

We first specify the setup in this section.
The central assumption is that the time-dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)=\sum_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}(t)
, defined on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, smoothly varies in time.
To be concrete, when we are interested in a
p
-th order PF during the time interval
[0,t]
, we assume that each
H_{\gamma}(\tau)
is of class
C^{p+2}
in
\tau\in[0,t]
.
Therefore, our results are applicable to broad types of time-dependence as well as some previous studies
[
62
,
13
]
.

When each Hamiltonian
H_{\gamma}(t)
is smooth, we can embed it into a time-periodic Hamiltonian.
Namely, there are series of time-periodic Hamiltonians
\{H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(t)\}
satisfying the following conditions (See Appendix
A
).

•

Periodicity: There exists a
\gamma
-independent period
T
, larger than the time of interest
t
, such that
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau)=H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau+T)
for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.
Each
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}
is expressed by a Fourier series,

H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{\gamma m}^{\mathrm{ex}}e^{-im\omega\tau},\quad\omega\equiv\frac{2\pi}{T},

(37)

where the Fourier coefficient
H_{\gamma m}^{\mathrm{ex}}
satisfies

\norm{H_{\gamma m}^\mr{ex}}\in\order{|m|^{-p-2}}.

(38)

•

Coincidence: Each
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau)
is equivalent to
H_{\gamma}(\tau)
in the interval
[0,t]
by

H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}(q)}(\tau)=H_{\gamma}^{(q)}(\tau),\quad\text{for $\tau\in[0,t]$},

(39)

for each
q=0,1,\cdots,p+2
.

The smoothness of
H(t)
appears as the decay of
H_{\gamma m}^{\mathrm{ex}}
by Eq. (
38
), which is required for the absolute and uniform convergence of the Fourier series of
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}(q)}(\tau)
for every
q=0,1,\cdots,p
.
This embedding enables us to employ the theory for analysing time-periodic Hamiltonians, called Floquet theory
[
45
]
, in order to obtain the error bound of the time-dependent PFs as we will discuss below.

While we will use the time-periodic Hamiltonian
H^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau)=\sum_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau)
during the derivation, the resulting error bound includes the information only in the interval
[0,t]
, which is equivalent to that for the original Hamiltonian
H(t)
.
Therefore, without loss of generality, we omit the superscript “ex” and deal with a generic smooth Hamiltonian
H(t)
as if each
H_{\gamma}(t)
is a time-periodic Hamiltonian satisfying

H_{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{\gamma m}e^{-im\omega t},\quad\norm{H_{\gamma m}}\in\order{|m|^{-p-2}}

(40)

with some period
T=2\pi/\omega
.
We note the Fourier series for its time derivatives,

H_{\gamma}^{(q)}(t)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}(-im\omega)^{q}H_{\gamma m}e^{-im\omega t},

(41)

is absolutely and uniformly convergent for any finite
t
up to
q=p
.
We use the notations for Floquet theory in Section
II.1
.

N
, the evolution time
t
, and the error tolerance
\varepsilon
. The extensiveness
g
defined by Eq. (
129
) satisfies
g\in\order{1}
for finite-range interactions or Eq. (
130
) for long-range interactions.
The quantity
f
represent the local scale of time-dependency by Eq. (
131
), which can be
\order{1}
for a broad class of local Hamiltonians in practice. The number of partitions in PFs,
\Gamma
, depends on the range of interactions as discussed in Section
VI.3
. The notation

contains polylogarithmic corrections with respect to
N
,
t
, or
1/\varepsilon
. See Definition
1
for the definition of uniform PFs.

III.2
Explicit error bound and improved cost

We summarize the central results of this paper, i.e., the error bound for time-dependent PFs explicitly organized by commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their time derivatives, and the improved cost reflecting the locality based on it.

We begin with the derivation of the time-dependent PF error.
The central strategy is to employ Floquet theory developed for time-periodic Hamiltonians.
The proof is divided into three steps.
First, we find a mapping of the time-dependent PF error to a time-independent one.
Floquet theory states that the time evolution
U(t,0)
can be expressed simply by the one under the time-independent Hamiltonian
H^{F}
[See Eq. (
33
)].
We find that a similar relation holds for the time-dependent PF errors in general.
For instance, we prove that the error of the standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
is expressed by

U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|[e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)]|0},

(42)

where
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
is a time-independent PF for
e^{-iH^{F}t}
defined on the infinite-dimensional space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{FH}}
.
The same goes also for the generalized PF
S(t,0)
.
As the second step, we employ the theory of the time-independent PF errors
[
12
]
for the mapped error like
e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)
.
We note that this does not immediately imply the commutator scaling due to the summation over
l\in\mathbb{Z}
in Eq. (
42
) and the unboundedness of
H^{F}
.
In the last step, we reduce the time-independent PF error on the infinite-dimensional space to the time-dependent PF error by carefully taking this summation.
We obtain the following error bounds as a first main result.

Theorem 2
.

(Commutator scaling, informal)

The
p
th-order standard and generalized PFs
\bar{S}(t,0)
and
S(t,0)
have the following error bounds,

\displaystyle\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\order{\max_{\tau\in[0,t]} \bar{\alpha}_{\mr{com},p}(\tau) \cdot t^{p+1}},

(43)

\displaystyle\norm{U(t,0)-S(t,0)}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\order{\max_{\tau\in[0,t]} \alpha_{\mr{com},p}(\tau) \cdot t^{p+1}},

(44)

where the factors
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
are defined by

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma_{0}=1}^{\Gamma}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{p}=1}^{\Gamma+1}\norm{\left[\prod_{q=1}^p \bar{\mcl{D}}_{\gamma_q}(\tau) \right] H_{\gamma_0}(\tau)},\quad\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\gamma}(\tau)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\gamma}(\tau)}&(\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma)\\
\Gamma\derivative{\tau}&(\gamma=\Gamma+1)\end{cases},

(45)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma_{0}=1}^{\Gamma}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{p}=1}^{\Gamma+1}\norm{\left[\prod_{q=1}^p \mcl{D}_{\gamma_q}(\tau) \right] H_{\gamma_0}(\tau)},\quad\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}(\tau)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\gamma}(\tau)}+i\derivative{\tau}&(\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma)\\
\Gamma\derivative{\tau}&(\gamma=\Gamma+1)\end{cases}.

(46)

These error bounds are composed of nested commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their time derivatives, while the latter one is inherent in time-dependent cases.
The advantage of our result is that they have many vanishing terms in the nested commutators when the Hamiltonian has the locality of interactions.
Indeed, we show that our error bounds achieve much better scaling in the system size
N
for generic time-dependent local Hamiltonians than the previous one with the
1
-norm scaling
[
62
]
.

A better error bound of a quantum algorithm implies better complexity of running it with a target accuracy.
We prove the improved cost of the time-dependent PFs based on the above error bounds as the second part of our main results, which is summarized in Table
1
.
Our result achieves better scaling in the system size
N
than the known results by the
1
-norm scaling error
[
62
]
.
We also note that it can be applied to generic local time-dependent Hamiltonians with finite-, short-, and long-ranged interactions in contrast to Ref.
[
13
]
, which shows the commutator-scaling error valid for finite-range interactions.
Furthermore, they can reproduce the best known results for the time-independent PFs obtained by the commutator-scaling errors
[
12
]
.

Finally, we also emphasize that our approach based on Floquet theory applies to a family of various time-dependent PFs.
The one for non-hermitian time-dependent Hamiltonians is the simplest example (See Appendix
D
).
In particular, the time-dependent multi-product formula (MPF), which uses a linear combination of time-dependent PFs, is a promising extension, with which we can exponentially improve the scaling in the desirable accuracy
[
37
,
1
,
43
]
.
As one of the main results, we derive the error bounds of the time-dependent MPFs which can reflect the locality of the Hamiltonian via nested commutators, and we prove the improved cost based on them as shown in Table
1
.
The time-dependent MPFs achieve the good
N
-scaling as small as the PFs and also achieve the good scaling in
t
and
\varepsilon
like the post-Trotter algorithms such as LCU and QSVT.

IV
Relationship between time-independent and -dependent Product formulas

In this section, we map the time-dependent PFs into the time-independent one in the infinite-dimensional Floquet-Hilbert space.

IV.1
PF in Floquet-Hilbert space

We begin by defining the time-independent PF in the Floquet-Hilbert space which can reproduce the time-dependent PF.
As introduced in Section
III
, the time evolution under
H(t)
can be organized by the Floquet Hamiltonian
H^{F}
[See Eq. (
33
)].
We note that, for smoothly varying Hamiltonians
H(t)
discussed in Sec.
III.1
, the infinite series for
U(t,t_{0})
in Eq. (
33
) is uniformly and absolutely convergent
[
40
]
.

We similarly construct a Floquet Hamiltonian
H_{\gamma}^{F}
for each term
H_{\gamma}(t)
by

H_{\gamma}^{F}=H^{\mathrm{Add}}_{\gamma}-H^{\mathrm{LP}},\quad H^{\mathrm{Add}}_{\gamma}=\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes H_{\gamma m},

(47)

and then, the time evolution under
H_{\gamma}(t)
is expressed by

U_{\gamma}(t+t_{0},t_{0})=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega(t+t_{0})}\braket{l|e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{F}t}|0}.

(48)

We define two kinds of time-independent PFs for
H^{F}
, which correspond to the standard and generalized PFs, respectively.

Definition 3
.

(PFs for Floquet Hamiltonian)

We define time-independent PFs
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
and
T^{F}(t)
, acting on the Floquet-Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{FH}}
, respectively by

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right),

(49)

\displaystyle T^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma}-\alpha_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{F}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right),

where we set
\beta_{v(\Gamma+1)}=\beta_{(v+1)1}
and
\beta_{V_{p}(\Gamma+1)}=1
.

We show that the time-independent PFs
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
and
T^{F}(t)
are related to the time-dependent PFs as follows.

Theorem 4
.

(Bridging PFs for original and Floquet Hamiltonians)

The standard and generalized PFs
\bar{S}(t,t_{0})
and
S(t,t_{0})
by Eqs. (
16
) and (
22
) are related to the time-independent PFs, Eqs. (
49
) and (
), by

\displaystyle\bar{S}(t,0)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|\bar{T}^{F}(t)|0},

(51)

\displaystyle S(t,0)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|T^{F}(t)|0},

(52)

when the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
satisfy Eqs. (
25
)-(
26
).

Proof.—

We focus on the standard PF.
We begin with showing that the time-evolution operator
e^{-iH_{\gamma}(\beta t)\alpha t}
for real
\alpha,\beta
is expressed by

e^{-iH_{\gamma}(\beta t)\alpha t}=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\beta t}\braket{l|e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha t}|0}.

(53)

We note that the right-hand side is absolutely and uniformly convergent, which immediately follows from
\|\braket{l|e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha t}|0}\|\in o(|l|^{-1})
by Lemma
A4
, which is shown in Appendix
A
.
Regarding
\alpha t
and
\beta t
as independent variables and fixing
\beta t
, we obtain

\displaystyle\derivative{(\alpha t)}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\beta t}\braket{l|e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha t}|0}

\displaystyle\quad=-i\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\beta t}\braket{l|H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha t}|0}

\displaystyle\quad=-i\sum_{l,m\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{\gamma m}e^{-i\{(l-m)+m\}\omega\beta t}\braket{l-m|e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha t}|0}

\displaystyle\quad=-iH_{\gamma}(\beta t)\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\beta t}\braket{l|e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha t}|0},

(54)

in which the exchange of the summation and the differentiation is allowed by the uniform convergence.
The second equality comes from Eq. (
47
).
In the last equality, we use the Fourier series Eq. (
40
) and its absolute convergence, which leads to
\sum_{l,m\in\mathbb{Z}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{l-m\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
We arrive at Eq. (
53
) by solving the differential equation Eq. (
54
).
Using the translation symmetry of
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}
by Eq. (
34
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{S}(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(\sum_{l_{v\gamma}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il_{v\gamma}\omega\beta_{v\gamma}t}\Braket{\sum_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\preceq v\gamma}l_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}|e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}|\sum_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\prec v\gamma}l_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}}\right)

(55)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(\sum_{l_{v\gamma}\in\mathbb{Z}}\Braket{\sum_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\preceq v\gamma}l_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}|e^{-iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{v\gamma}t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{v\gamma}t}|\sum_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\prec v\gamma}l_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\Braket{l|e^{-iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{V_{p}(\Gamma+1)}t}\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right)e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{11}t}|0},

where we used the completeness
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\ket{l}\bra{l}=I
in the last equality.
We note that this calculation is validated by the absolute convergence of the infinite series, Eq. (
53
), which allows us to change the way of taking the summation over
\{l_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
This completes the proof of Eq. (
51
) by the definition of
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
, Eq. (
49
).

The proof of Eq. (
52
) is parallel to the above calculation.
We substitute the expression of
U_{\gamma}(t,0)
by Eq. (
48
), and then we use the translation symmetry of
e^{-iH^{F}t}
by Eq. (
36
).
We arrive at Eq. (
52
) as the expression of the generalized PF
S(t,0)
.
\quad\square

Theorem
4
shows a simple relation between time-independent and -dependent PFs.
Instead of discarding the time dependence, the PFs acquire infinite dimensionality as well as the exact time evolution, Eq. (
33
).
Consequently, the errors of the time-dependent PFs can be attributed to those of the time-independent PFs
e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)
or
e^{-iH^{F}t}-T^{F}(t)
like Eq. (
42
).
This is the origin of the commutator scaling of the time-dependent PFs.
However, we emphasize that the presence of the commutator scaling in the time-dependent PF errors is nontrivial at the present stage due to the infinite-dimensionality.
The Hamiltonians
H_{\gamma}^{F}
and
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
are both unbounded, and the summation over
l\in\mathbb{Z}
does not necessarily provides a meaningful bound.
The explicit computation of Eq. (
42
) in Section
V
is essential for concluding the commutator scaling.

We remark several points before finishing this section.
First, in order to satisfy the order condition
\bar{S}(t,0)=U(t,0)+\order{t^{p+1}}
or
S(t,0)=U(t,0)+\order{t^{p+1}}
, we will demand that
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
or
T^{F}(t)
become
p
th-order time-independent PFs for
e^{-iH^{F}t}
.
The difference between the standard and generalized PFs lies in the way of the decomposition of the Floquet Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle H^{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}-H^{\mathrm{LP}}

(56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}^{F}+(\Gamma-1)H^{\mathrm{LP}}.

(57)

The decomposition by the first line corresponds to the standard PF, which employs the time-evolution operators under
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}
and
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
in
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
.
Similarly, the one by the second line corresponds to the generalized PF.
These decompositions will be used for determining the proper coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
.

We also note that the assumption
\beta_{11}=0
affects the construction of the operators
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
and
T^{F}(t)
via this order condition.
Equations (
49
) and (
) cannot provide a
p
th-order time-independent PF for
e^{-iH^{F}t}
under
\beta_{11}=0
.
When we have
\beta_{11}\neq 0
, the operator
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
is replaced by

\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right)e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{11}t}

(58)

and so does the operator
T^{F}(t)
similarly.
The calculation in the following sections is a little bit modified, while the scalings of the error bounds and the computational cost do not essentially alter.
See Appendix
B.2
for the detailed discussion.

Finally, we provide the translation symmetry of the time-independent PFs
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
and
T^{F}(t)
like Eq. (
36
), which will be necessary for the calculation in the next section.

Lemma 5
.

(Translation symmetry of PFs)

The time-independent PFs
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
and
T^{F}(t)
by Definition
3
satisfy the translation symmetry,

\displaystyle\braket{l|\bar{T}^{F}(t)|l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il^{\prime\prime}\omega t}\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|\bar{T}^{F}(t)|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}},

(59)

\displaystyle\braket{l|T^{F}(t)|l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il^{\prime\prime}\omega t}\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|T^{F}(t)|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}},

(60)

for every
l,l^{\prime},l^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.—

Suppose that operators
A^{F}
and
B^{F}
, acting on the Floquet-Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{FH}}
, satisfy the symmetry,

\displaystyle\braket{l|A^{F}|l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il^{\prime\prime}a}\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|A^{F}|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}},

(61)

\displaystyle\braket{l|B^{F}|l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il^{\prime\prime}b}\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|B^{F}|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}},

(62)

for every
l,l^{\prime},l^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
with certain values
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\braket{l|A^{F}B^{F}|l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|A^{F}|l^{\prime\prime\prime}}\braket{l^{\prime\prime\prime}|B^{F}|l^{\prime}}

(63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{il^{\prime\prime}(a+b)}\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|A^{F}|l^{\prime\prime\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\braket{l^{\prime\prime\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}|B^{F}|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il^{\prime\prime}(a+b)}\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|A^{F}B^{F}|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}}.

We repeat this relation based on the symmetry of
H^{\mathrm{Add}}_{\gamma}
and
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
by Eqs. (
34
) and (
35
) for the standard PF
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
, which results in

\displaystyle\braket{l|\bar{T}^{F}(t)|l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il^{\prime\prime}\omega\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t}

(64)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|\bar{T}^{F}(t)|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il^{\prime\prime}\omega t}\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|\bar{T}^{F}(t)|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}}.

We used
\beta_{11}=0
and
\beta_{V_{p}(\Gamma+1)}=1
in the last equality.
The symmetry for the generalized PF
T^{F}(t)
is shown by using that for
e^{-iH^{F}t}
by Eq. (
36
) in a similar manner.
\quad\square

While the PFs
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
and
T^{F}(t)
are constructed as approximate formulas of
e^{-iH^{F}t}
, Lemma
5
implies that the translation symmetry of
e^{-iH^{F}t}
is exactly inherited.
This relation will be used to obtain the error representation of the time-dependent PF errors.

IV.2
Example: Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula

In this section, we consider the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formulas as a concrete example, and we show that those for time-dependent systems are completely expressed by those for time-independent cases via Theorem
4
.
We begin with the first-order formulas
\bar{S}_{1}(t,0)
and
S(t,0)
, in which we have
V_{1}=1
,
\alpha_{v\gamma}=1
, and
\beta_{v\gamma}=0
.
The corresponding time-independent PFs based on Definition
3
are given by

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}t}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}t},

(65)

\displaystyle T^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-iH_{\Gamma}^{F}t}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{-iH^{\mathrm{LP}}t}e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{F}t}\right).

(66)

The former one is just the first-order Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula with the decomposition,
H^{F}=H_{1}^{\mathrm{Add}}+\cdots+H_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
.
The latter one comes from the decomposition,
H^{F}=H_{1}^{F}+H^{\mathrm{LP}}+H_{2}^{F}+\cdots+H^{\mathrm{LP}}+H_{\Gamma}^{F}
.
These relations hold for generic orders
p
, which is described by the following theorem.

Theorem 6
.

We define the time-independent PF
\bar{T}_{p}^{F}(t)
by Eq. (
65
) for
p=1
,
\bar{T}_{2}^{F}(t)=\bar{T}_{1}^{F}(-t/2)^{\dagger}\bar{T}_{1}^{F}(t/2)
for
p=2
, and

T_{2q}^{F}(t)=[T_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)]^{2}T_{2q-2}^{F}(b_{q}t)^{\dagger}[T_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)]^{2}

(67)

for
q\in\mathbb{N}+1
.
The
p
th-order Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula
\bar{S}_{p}(t,0)
is expressed by

\bar{S}_{p}(t,0)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|\bar{T}_{p}^{F}(t)|0}.

(68)

We also have

S_{p}(t,0)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|T_{p}^{F}(t)|0},

(69)

where
T_{p}^{F}(t)
is constructed recursively beginning with Eq. (
66
) in a similar manner.

Proof.—

We focus on the standard PF with the order
p\geq 2
.
Based on the definition of
\bar{S}_{2}(t,0)
by Eqs. (
16
) and (
17
), we have
V_{2}=2
,
\alpha_{1\gamma}=\alpha_{2\gamma}=1/2
,
\beta_{1\gamma}=0
, and
\beta_{2\gamma}=1
.
The permutation
\pi_{v}
is given by Eq. (
10
).
The time-independent PF defined by Eq. (
49
) is equal to

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{\gamma=\Gamma}^{1}e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}t/2}\right)e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}t}\left(\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}e^{-iH_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}t/2}\right)

(70)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{T}_{1}^{F}(-t/2)^{\dagger}\bar{T}_{1}^{F}(t/2).

Thus, Theorem
4
implies the satisfaction of Eq. (
68
) for
p=2
.
Next, we assume Eq. (
68
) for
p=2q-2
.
The recursive relation Eq. (
67
) implies

\displaystyle[\text{r.h.s. of Eq. (\ref{Eq_Rel:Rel_LST_Std})}]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{l,l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|l_{1}}\braket{l_{1}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|l_{2}}\braket{l_{2}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(b_{q}t)^{\dagger}|l_{3}}\braket{l_{3}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|l_{4}}\braket{l_{4}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|l_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{l,l_{1},l_{2},l_{3},l_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-i(l-l_{1})\omega t}\braket{l-l_{1}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|0}e^{-i(l_{1}-l_{2})\omega(1-a_{q})t}\braket{l_{1}-l_{2}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|0}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times\left(e^{-i(l_{3}-l_{2})\omega(1-2a_{q})t}\braket{l_{3}-l_{2}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(b_{q}t)|0}\right)^{\dagger}e^{-i(l_{3}-l_{4})\omega(2a_{q})t}\braket{l_{3}-l_{4}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|0}e^{-il_{4}\omega a_{q}t}\braket{l_{4}|\bar{T}_{2q-2}^{F}(a_{q}t)|0}

\displaystyle\quad=\bar{S}_{2q-2}(t,t-a_{q}t)\bar{S}_{2q-2}(t-a_{q}t,t-2a_{q}t)\bar{S}_{2q-2}(2a_{q}t,t-2a_{q}t)^{\dagger}\bar{S}_{2q-2}(2a_{q}t,a_{q}t)\bar{S}_{2q-2}(a_{q}t,0),

(71)

where we used the completeness in the first equality, and translation symmetry by Lemma
5
in the second equality.
The definition of
\bar{S}_{2q}(t,0)
by Eq. (
18
) completes the proof of Eq. (
68
) by induction.
The same discussion goes also for the generalized PF.
\quad\square

To summarize, Theorem
6
implies that the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formulas for time-dependent Hamiltonian are completely translated into those for time-independent Hamiltonians.
The appearance of the different types of the PFs, i,e., the standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
and the generalized PF
S(t,0)
, can be attributed to the ways of the decomposition of
H^{F}
.

V
Error bound of time-dependent product formulas

We derive the error bounds for the time-dependent PFs, which shows the commutator scaling.
As shown in the previous section, the time-dependent PF error can be translated into the time-independent one for the Floquet Hamiltonian.
We first clarify the error representation by applying the technique for time-independent systems
[
12
]
in Section
V.1
.
We have to be aware that the time-independent counterpart is defined on the infinite-dimensional Floquet-Hilbert space, and that this does not immediately provide a meaningful error bound.
In Section
V.3
, we explicitly compute the summation over
l\in\mathbb{Z}
and show a convergent error bound, which results in the meaningful commutator scaling of the time-dependent PF error.
Section
V.2
is devoted to computing commutators appearing in the errors in Section
V.3
, which gives the origin of the errors expressed by the nested commutators and their time derivatives.
Throughout this section, we concentrate on deriving the error bound of the standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
.
While we briefly discuss the results for the generalized PF
S(t,0)
, we provide their detailed derivation in Appendix
B
.

V.1
Error representation

Section
IV
proves that the error of the time-dependent PF can be translated into the one of the time-independent counterpart by Eq. (
42
) defined on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{FH}}
.
Next, we apply the theory of PF errors for time-independent Hamiltonians
[
12
]
to this.
Focusing on the standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
, we assume that its counterpart
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
by Eq. (
49
) formally gives a
p
th-order time-independent PF for
e^{-iH^{F}t}
.
We associate
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}
and
-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
respectively with
H_{\gamma}
and
H_{0}
of a Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}
, and then it is equivalent to consider the time-independent PF,

\bar{T}(t)=\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{-iH_{0}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right),

(72)

satisfying the order condition
\bar{T}(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
.
The error
e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)
can be represented completely in the same way as
e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}(t)
.

We introduce some useful notations before give the error representation based on the above strategy.
The time-independent PF
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
by Eq. (
49
) can be rewritten as

\bar{T}^{F}(t)\equiv\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}t},

(73)

where
\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
are respectively given by

(\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}})=\begin{cases}(H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}},\alpha_{v\gamma})&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
(-H^{\mathrm{LP}},\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma)\end{cases}.

(74)

The coefficient
\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
has an absolute value smaller than
1
owing to Eqs. (
25
)-(
26
).
We also define the partial product of
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
by

\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(t)\equiv\prod_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}=11}^{v(\tilde{\gamma}-1)}e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}t}.

(75)

With these notations, we obtain the following error representation.

Lemma 7
.

(Error representation)

Suppose that the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
are chosen so that the time-independent PF
\bar{T}(t)
by Eq. (
49
) can satisfy the order condition
\bar{T}(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
for a Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}
.
The difference
e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)
is given by

e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)=-i\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau e^{-iH^{F}(t-\tau)}\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau),

(76)

where the operator
\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)
is defined by

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\frac{i^{p}(\tau-\tau_{1})^{q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}-1}\tau^{p-q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}}{(q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}-1)!q_{v(\tilde{\gamma}+1)}!\cdots q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}!}C_{1}(H^{F})

(77)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{v\tilde{\gamma}\preceq v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\frac{i^{p}(\tau-\tau_{1})^{q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}-1}\tau^{p-q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}}{(q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}-1)!q_{v(\tilde{\gamma}+1)}!\cdots q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}!}C_{2}(\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle C_{1}(H^{F})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)^{\dagger}e^{i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\left[\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}=V_{p}2\Gamma}^{v\tilde{\gamma}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}\right)^{q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}H^{F}\right]e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau),

(78)

\displaystyle C_{2}(\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)^{\dagger}e^{i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\left[\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}=v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{v\tilde{\gamma}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}\right)^{q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\right]e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau).

(79)

Proof.—

Since the representation Eq. (
73
) is essentially the same as Eq. (
7
), we can directly apply the approach for time-independent cases (See Theorem 5 in Ref.
[
12
]
).
We begin by noticing that the difference is represented by

e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)=-e^{-iH^{F}t}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\derivative{\tau}e^{iH^{F}\tau}\bar{T}^{F}(\tau).

(80)

This implies that we have Eq. (
76
) with

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)^{\dagger}e^{-iH^{F}\tau}\derivative{\tau}e^{iH^{F}\tau}\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)^{\dagger}H^{F}\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}=1}^{2\Gamma}\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\bar{T}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{F}(\tau)^{\dagger}\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\bar{T}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{F}(\tau).

In the above equation, both of the two terms have the form,

\left(\prod_{k=1}^{K}e^{\tau\mathrm{ad}_{A_{k}}}\right)B=e^{A_{K}\tau}\cdots e^{A_{1}\tau}Be^{-A_{1}\tau}\cdots e^{-A_{K}\tau},

(82)

with some operators
\{A_{k}\}
and
B
.
We repeatedly use the Taylor’s theorem,

\displaystyle e^{\tau\mathrm{ad}_{A_{k}}}B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{q=0}^{p-1}\frac{(\tau\mathrm{ad}_{A_{k}})^{q}}{q!}B

(83)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}e^{\tau_{1}\mathrm{ad}_{A_{k}}}\frac{(\tau_{1}\mathrm{ad}_{A_{k}})^{p}}{p!}B,

for
k=1,\cdots,K
so that the Taylor’s reminder can be
\order{\tau^p}
.
This results in the following formula, which is used for time-independent PFs
[
12
]
,

\left(\prod_{k=1}^{K}e^{\tau\mathrm{ad}_{A_{k}}}\right)B=\sum_{q=0}^{p-1}\mathcal{A}_{q}\tau^{q}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}+\cdots+q_{k}=p\\
q_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\frac{(\tau-\tau_{1})^{q_{k}-1}\tau^{p-q_{k}}}{(q_{k}-1)!q_{k-1}!\cdots q_{1}!}\left(\prod_{k^{\prime}=k+1}^{K}e^{\tau\mathrm{ad}_{H_{k^{\prime}}}}\right)e^{\tau_{1}\mathrm{ad}_{H_{k}}}\left(\prod_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}(\mathrm{ad}_{H_{k^{\prime}}})^{q_{k^{\prime}}}B\right).

(84)

When we apply this formula to the first and the second terms in Eq. (
), the first term of Eq. (
84
) vanishes due to the order condition
\tilde{T}(t)=e^{-iH^{F}t}+\order{t^{p+1}}
just like the time-independent PF.
The remaining second term of Eq. (
84
) give Eq. (
77
) as the form of
\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)
, which completes the proof.
\quad\square

In the case of the time-independent PFs, the counterparts of Eqs. (
78
) and (
79
) directly give the commutator scaling of the errors
[
12
]
.
We have a certain operator
\Delta(\tau)
similar to Eq. (
77
), such that

e^{-iHt}-T(t)=-i\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau e^{-iH(t-\tau)}T(\tau)\Delta(\tau).

(85)

This immediately implies the error bound,

\norm{e^{-iHt}-T(t)}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\norm{\Delta(\tau)},

(86)

and the operator norm
\norm{\Delta(\tau)}
characterizes the error bound.
The representation of
\Delta(\tau)
similar to Eq. (
77
) indicates the scaling
\Delta(\tau)\in\order{\tau^p}
and the commutator scaling by the counterpart of Eqs. (
78
) and (
79
).
As a result, the time-independent PF error of
\order{\alpha_{\mr{com},p} t^{p+1}}
is obtained.

In time-dependent cases, Eqs. (
78
) and (
79
) are the origins of the commutator scaling for the time-dependent PF errors, although evaluating its error bound is not so simple.
The difficulty mainly comes from the facts that the error
e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)
is defined on the infinite-dimensional space.
We have to consider the infinite summation over
l\in\mathbb{Z}
to reproduce the error in the original space as follows.

Theorem 8
.

The additive error of the time-dependent PF error is expressed by

\displaystyle U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)=

\displaystyle\qquad-i\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau U(t,\tau)\bar{S}(\tau,0)\left(\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}\right),

(87)

where the operator
\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)
is given by Eqs. (
77
)-(
79
).

Proof.—

Using the error representation by Eq. (
76
), the error of interest is computed as follows;

\displaystyle U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)

\displaystyle\quad=-i\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\braket{l|e^{-iH^{F}(t-\tau)}\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}

\displaystyle\quad=-i\sum_{l,l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|e^{-iH^{F}(t-\tau)}|l_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\times\braket{l_{1}|\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)|l_{2}}\braket{l_{2}|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}

\displaystyle\quad=-i\sum_{l,l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau e^{-i(l-l_{1})\omega t}\braket{l-l_{1}|e^{-iH^{F}(t-\tau)}|0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times e^{-i(l_{1}-l_{2})\omega\tau}\braket{l_{1}-l_{2}|\bar{T}^{F}(\tau)|0}\braket{l_{2}|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0},

(88)

where we use the translation symmetry [Eq. (
36
)] and Lemma
5
] in the third equality.
The integrand of the last line forms a Cauchy product of the infinite series for
U(t,\tau)
[Eq. (
33
)],
\bar{S}(\tau,0)
[Eq. (
51
)], and
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}
.
While the former two series are absolutely convergent, the last one
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}
is convergent as we will compute in Sections
V.2
-
V.3
.
Then, we can use Mertens’ theorem
[
30
]
, which implies the convergence of Eq. (
88
) to the right-hand side of Eq. (
87
).
\quad\square

As a consequence, the operator norm of the PF error is bounded by

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\sum_{l \in\bbZ} \braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^F(\tau)|0}}

(89)

for time-dependent cases instead of Eq. (
86
), and this difference brings the difficulty of dealing with time dependence.
While each matrix element of
\Delta^{F}(\tau)
is expected to be
\order{\tau^p}
and to have a coefficient with the commutator scaling, it is nontrivial whether these properties are maintained after infinite summation over
l\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We also note that the time-dependent PF error is an infinite series composed of the nested commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\}_{\gamma}
and
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
via Eqs. (
78
) and (
79
).
It should be examined whether it provides a meaningful bounded value since the latter one is unbounded, and it is also of importance how it is related to nested commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}_{\gamma}
.
To solve these problems, we have to compute the explicit form of
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}
.

V.2
Origin of commutator scaling

The next step for the explicit error bound is to compute the operator
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}
in detail.
In this section, we calculate nested commutators appearing in
\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)
, which will be proven to be the origins of nested commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}_{\gamma}
and their time derivatives in the error of time-dependent PFs.

Let us consider the following term appearing in Eqs. (
78
) and (
79
),

\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}=v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{v\tilde{\gamma}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}\right)^{q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}\tilde{H},

(90)

for
v\tilde{\gamma}\preceq v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
, where
\tilde{H}
is chosen from either
\{H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\}_{\gamma}
or
-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
.
Since each
\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}
is chosen from the same set by Eq. (
74
), it is the nested commutator among
\{H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}\}_{\gamma}
or
-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
.
We show that it can be related to nested commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}_{\gamma}
and their time derivatives as follows.

Lemma 9
.

We define an operation
\bar{D}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(t)
by

\bar{D}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(t)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(t)}&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
i\derivative{t}&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma)\end{cases},

(91)

for
\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma
.
We also use
(f(t))_{m}
to represent the Fourier coefficient of a time-dependent function
f(t)
, given by

(f(t))_{m}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}f(t^{\prime})e^{im\omega t^{\prime}}.

(92)

The commutators with
\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(\bar{D}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(t)H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)\right)_{m},

(93)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H^{\mathrm{LP}}

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(i\derivative{t}H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(t)\right)_{m}&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
\displaystyle 0&(\text{otherwise})\end{cases},

(94)

for
\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma
.

Proof.—

We first consider Eq. (
93
).
For an odd integer
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}},H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}]

(95)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m+m^{\prime}}\otimes[H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)m},H_{\gamma^{\prime}m^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\sum_{m^{\prime}}[H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)(m-m^{\prime})},H_{\gamma^{\prime}m^{\prime}}].

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(t)}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)\right)_{m}.

The last equality is based on the fact that the Fourier coefficient of the commutator
[H_{\gamma}(t),H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)]
is expressed by

\displaystyle([H_{\gamma}(t),H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)])_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}[H_{\gamma}(t),H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)]e^{im\omega t}\differential t

(96)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m^{\prime}}[H_{\gamma(m-m^{\prime})},H_{\gamma^{\prime}m^{\prime}}].

For an even integer
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[-H^{\mathrm{LP}},H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}]

(97)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{m}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}l\omega[\ket{l}\bra{l},\mathrm{Add}_{m}]\otimes H_{\gamma^{\prime}m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes(m\omega H_{\gamma^{\prime}m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(i\derivative{t}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)\right)_{m}.

This completes the proof of Eq. (
93
).
It is easy to confirm Eq. (
94
).
We have
\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H^{\mathrm{LP}}=-\mathrm{ad}_{H^{\mathrm{LP}}}H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}
for odd
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1
, which is similar to Eq. (
97
).
It is trivially
0
for even
\tilde{\gamma}
due to
\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}=-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
.
\quad\square

This lemma indicates that the commutator with
H^{\mathrm{Add}}_{\gamma}
in the Floquet-Hilbert space is replaced by the one with
H_{\gamma}(t)
, defined on the original space.
The commutator with
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
is converted to the time derivative.
We next consider the expression of Eq. (
90
).
Lemma
9
states that the application of
\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}
to
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}=\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes(H_{\gamma}(t))_{m}
merely transforms its Fourier coefficient
(H_{\gamma}(t))_{m}
.
Thus, we can use this lemma again when further applying
\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}
.
Equation (
77
) for
\tilde{H}=H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}
is expressed by

\displaystyle\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}=v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{v\tilde{\gamma}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}\right)^{q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\gamma}(t)\right)_{m},

(98)

\displaystyle C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\gamma}(t)=\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}=v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{v\tilde{\gamma}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\bar{D}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}(t)\right)^{q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}H_{\gamma}(t).

(99)

In the case of
\tilde{H}=-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
, let us define the maximal nontrivial index by

(v_{\mathrm{M}},\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{M}})=\max\left[(v^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime})\text{ s.t. }q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\neq 0\right].

(100)

Using Eq. (
94
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}=v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{v\tilde{\gamma}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}\right)^{q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}(-H^{\mathrm{LP}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(t)\right)_{m},

(101)

where the operator
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(t)
is defined by

\displaystyle C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(t)

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}=v_{\mathrm{M}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}+1)}^{v\tilde{\gamma}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}\bar{D}_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}(t)\right)^{q_{v^{\prime\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{v_{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}}\bar{D}_{v_{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}}(t)\right)^{q_{v_{\mathrm{M}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}}-1}\left(-i\derivative{t}H_{\gamma_{\mathrm{M}}}(t)\right)

for an odd integer
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}=2\gamma_{\mathrm{M}}-1
and
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(t)=0
for an even integer
\tilde{\gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}=2\gamma_{\mathrm{M}}
.

We next show that the operators
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\gamma}(t)
and
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(t)
directly appear the error bounds of the time-dependent PFs.
Namely, they are the origins of the commutator scaling involving the nested commutators
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their time derivative.
First, we prove the following lemma for calculation.

Lemma 10
.

Let
\bar{S}_{\prec v\gamma}(t,0)
be the partial standard PF defined by

\bar{S}_{\prec v\gamma}(t,0)=\prod_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}=11}^{v(\gamma-1)}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime})}(\beta_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}t)\alpha_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}t}.

(103)

When the time
\tau_{1}
is in
[0,\tau]
, there exists certain time
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})\in[0,\tau]
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})}\braket{l|e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)|0}

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{cases}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}\tau)\alpha_{v\gamma}\tau_{1}}\bar{S}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,0)&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
\bar{S}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,0)&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma)\end{cases}

(104)

is satisfied.
In addition, it has the translation symmetry,

\displaystyle\braket{l-l^{\prime\prime}|e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)|l^{\prime}-l^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=e^{il^{\prime\prime}\omega\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})}\braket{l|e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)|l^{\prime}},

(105)

satisfied for every
l,l^{\prime},l^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.—

We consider an odd integer
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1
.
In a similar manner to the proof of Theorem
4
, we obtain the relation,

\bar{S}_{\prec v\gamma}(\tau,0)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\beta_{v\gamma}\tau}\braket{l|\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)|0}.

(106)

We set the time
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})=\beta_{v\gamma}\tau\in[0,\tau]
.
Using the translation symmetry by Eq. (
34
) and the relation Eq. (
53
) results in

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})}\braket{l|e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)|0}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{l,l_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\beta_{v\gamma}\tau}\braket{l-l_{1}|e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha_{v\gamma}\tau_{1}}|0}\braket{l_{1}|\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)|0}

\displaystyle\quad=e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}\tau)\alpha_{v\gamma}\tau_{1}}\bar{S}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,0).

(107)

We next consider the case where
\tilde{\gamma}
is an even integer by
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma
.
We set time
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})=\beta_{v\gamma}\tau+(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})\tau_{1}
, which lies between
\beta_{v\gamma}\tau
and
\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}\tau
, and hence it is included in
[0,\tau]
.
The same calculation leads to the satisfaction of Eq. (
104
).
The symmetry by Eq. (
105
) immediately follows from Lemma
5
.
\quad\square

As discussed in Eq. (
89
), the error bound is determined by
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}
.
Using the explicit formula of
\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)
by Eq. (
77
), it is further represented by

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|C_{1}(H^{F})|0}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|C_{1}(H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}})|0}-\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|C_{1}(H^{\mathrm{LP}})|0},

(108)

and also by
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|C_{2}(\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}})|0}
similarly.
The first term can be calculated as follows.
For an odd integer
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|C_{1}(H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}})|0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l,l_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\bra{l}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)^{\dagger}e^{i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\left[\sum_{m}\ket{l_{1}+m}\bra{l_{1}}\otimes\left(C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma^{\prime}}(t)\right)_{m}\right]e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)\ket{0}

(109)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l,l_{1},m}\bra{l-m}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)^{\dagger}e^{i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\ket{l_{1}}\left(C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma^{\prime}}(t)\right)_{m}e^{-im\omega\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})}\bra{l_{1}}e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l,l_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})}\bra{0}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)^{\dagger}e^{i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\ket{l_{1}-l}C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma^{\prime}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1}))\bra{l_{1}}e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}\bar{T}^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{S}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,0)^{\dagger}e^{iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}\tau)\alpha_{v\gamma}\tau_{1}}C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma^{\prime}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1}))e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}\tau)\alpha_{v\gamma}\tau_{1}}\bar{S}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,0).

The first equality comes from Eq. (
98
).
We use Lemma
10
for the third and last equalities.
As a result, the norm of Eq. (
109
) is bounded by

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\sum_{l \in\bbZ} \braket{l|C_1(H_{\gamma'}^\mr{Add})|0}}\leq\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p 2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma'}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))},

(110)

which is valid also for even
\tilde{\gamma}
due to Lemma
10
.
We also obtain

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\sum_{l \in\bbZ} \braket{l|C_1(H^\mr{LP})|0}}\leq\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p 2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\mr{LP}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))},

(111)

and the similar relations hold for the norm of
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|C_{2}(\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}})|0}
as well.

To summarize, all the terms included in the norm of
\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)|0}
, which determines the error bound, can be expressed by those of
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\gamma^{\prime}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1}))
and
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1}))
.
This is why the nested commutators among
H_{\gamma}(t)
and their time derivative appear in the error bound of the time-dependent PFs.
We remark that the time
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})
is always included in
[0,t]
.
We extrapolate the Hamiltonian
H(t)
for
\mathbb{R}\backslash[0,t]
as discussed in Section
III.1
, and its Floquet Hamiltonian
H^{F}
contains the information for all the time including the extrapolated domain
\mathbb{R}\backslash[0,t]
, which is irrelevant for the time evolution
U(t,0)
.
The fact
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})\in[0,t]
indicates that the nested commutators and their time derivatives only during
[0,t]
determine the PF errors independent of the extrapolation.

V.3
Explicit error bound

Now, we are ready to derive the concrete error bound for generic time-dependent PFs by summarizing the results so far.
The error bound of the standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
is given by the following theorem.

Theorem 11
.

(Error of the standard PF)

Suppose that the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
are chosen so that the time-independent PF
\bar{T}(t)
by Eq. (
72
) can satisfy the order condition
\bar{T}(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
.
The standard PF error has an upper bound by

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)}\leq 4(V_{p}t)^{p+1}\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau),

(112)

where the quantity
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
is defined by Eq. (
45
).

Proof.—

Combining the discussion in Eqs. (
108
)-(
111
) with the expression of
\bar{\Delta}^{F}(\tau)
by Eq. (
77
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\sum_{l \in\bbZ} \braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^F(\tau)|0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tau^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p 2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma'}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))}+\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p 2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\mr{LP}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))}\right)

(113)

\displaystyle\qquad+\tau^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}:\text{odd}}\sum_{v\tilde{\gamma}\preceq v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v'\tilde{\gamma}'}^{\{q\},\frac{\tilde{\gamma}'+1}2}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\tau^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}:\text{even}}\sum_{v\tilde{\gamma}\preceq v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq 0:\\
