q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v'\tilde{\gamma}'}^{\{q\},\mr{LP}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))}.

Owing to
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})\in[0,\tau]
, the integral in the above equation can be replaced by

\tau^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}f(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1}))\leq\tau^{p}\max_{\tau_{1}\in[0,\tau]}f(\tau_{1}),

(114)

where
f(\tau_{1})
denotes the summations appearing in the integrand.

We first focus on the summation of
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma^{\prime}}(\tau_{1})
, which appears in the first term of Eq. (
113
).
Since the indices
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}
satisfy the constraint
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}=p
, each of
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma^{\prime}}(\tau_{1})
by Eq. (
99
) has the form

\left[\prod_{q=1}^{p}\tilde{\alpha}_{v_{q}\tilde{\gamma}_{q}}\bar{D}_{v_{q}\tilde{\gamma}_{q}}(\tau_{1})\right]H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\tau_{1})

(115)

with some indices
\{v_{q}\tilde{\gamma}_{q}\}_{q=1}^{p}
.
Since all the terms appearing in the summation over the indices
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}
are covered by the one over all the possible indices
\{v_{q}\tilde{\gamma}_{q}\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma'}(\tau_1)}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{11},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}\geq 0:\\
q_{11}+\cdots+q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}=p\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{11,V_p2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma'}(\tau_1)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{p}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\gamma}_{p}=1}^{2\Gamma}\norm{\left[\prod_{q=1}^p \bar{D}_{v_q\tilde{\gamma}_q}(\tau_1) \right] H_{\gamma'}(\tau_1)},

where we use
|\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}|\leq 1
as discussed in Eq. (
74
).
Since the
\Gamma
copies of the derivative
\derivative{\tau_1}
by even
\tilde{\gamma}
can be replaced by
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\Gamma+1}(\tau_{1})=\Gamma\derivative{\tau_1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma'}(\tau_1)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq(V_{p})^{p}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau_{1}),

(117)

according to the definition, Eq. (
45
).
The same goes also for the summation of
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}+1}{2}}(\tau_{1})
, which results in

\displaystyle\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}:\text{odd}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\tilde{\gamma}\\
\,\preceq v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v'\tilde{\gamma}'}^{\{q\},\frac{\tilde{\gamma}'+1}2}(\tau_1)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq(V_{p})^{p+1}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau_{1}).

(118)

The additional factor
V_{p}
comes from the summation over
v^{\prime}
from
1
to
V_{p}
.

The quantity
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(\tau_{1})
in Eq. (
113
) is evaluated in a similar manner, whose summation over the indices
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}
can be replaced by the one over
\{v_{q}\tilde{\gamma}_{q}\}_{q=2}^{p}
as well.
While the last index is fixed to
\gamma_{\mathrm{M}}
defined by Eq. (
100
), we can delete it by using a trivial relation,

\norm{(\cdots) \dv{t}H_{\gamma_\mr{M}}(\tau_1)}\leq\sum_{\gamma_{0}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{(\cdots) \dv{t}H_{\gamma_0}(\tau_1)}.

(119)

As a result, the summation of
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_{p}2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(\tau_{1})
appearing in the first term of Eq. (
113
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{V_{p}2\Gamma}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p 2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\mr{LP}}(\tau_1)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{v_{2}\tilde{\gamma}_{2},\cdots,v_{p}\tilde{\gamma}_{p}=11}^{V_{p}2\Gamma}\sum_{\gamma_{0}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{\left[\prod_{q=2}^p \bar{D}_{v_q\tilde{\gamma}_q}(\tau_1) \right] \dv{t} H_{\gamma_0}(\tau_1)},

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{(V_{p})^{p-1}}{\Gamma}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau_{1}).

(120)

In the second inequality, we use
\derivative{\tau}=\Gamma^{-1}\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\Gamma+1}(\tau)
and the fact that it is covered by the summation of
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\gamma_{1}}(\tau)
over
\gamma_{1}
from
1
to
\Gamma+1
.
Similarly, the summation of
C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}^{\{q\},\mathrm{LP}}(\tau_{1})
is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}:\text{even}}\sum_{v\tilde{\gamma}=11}^{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{v^{\prime}2\gamma^{\prime}}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{v^{\prime}2\gamma^{\prime}}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v'\tilde{\gamma}'}^{\{q\},\mr{LP}}(\tau_1)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq(V_{p})^{p}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau_{1}),

(121)

where the additional factor
V_{p}\Gamma
compared to Eq. (
120
) comes from the summation over
v^{\prime}
and even
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

Finally, summarizing the relations, Eqs. (
113
)-(
121
), we obtain

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\sum_{l \in\bbZ} \braket{l|\bar{\Delta}^F(\tau)|0}}\leq 4(V_{p}\tau)^{p+1}\max_{\tau_{1}\in[0,\tau]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau_{1}).

(122)

Equation (
89
) immediately leads to Eq. (
112
), which completes the proof.

\quad\square

Theorem
11
is the central result of this paper, which gives the time-dependent PF error explicitly composed of the commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their time-derivatives.
Similarly, the error bound of the generalized PF
S(t,0)
is given by the following theorem, which completes Theorem
2
in Section
III
with Theorem
11
.

Theorem 12
.

(Error of the generalized PF)

Suppose that the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma}\}
and
\{\beta_{v\gamma}\}
are chosen so that the time-independent PF,

T(t)=\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH_{0}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma}-\alpha_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right)

(123)

satisfies the order condition
T(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
for
H=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}+(\Gamma-1)H_{0}
.
The generalized PF error has an upper bound by

\norm{U(t,0)-S(t,0)}\leq 5(V_{p}t)^{p+1}\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau),

(124)

where the quantity
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
is defined by Eq. (
46
).

We provide the detailed proof in Appendix
B
.
The difference from the standard PF is the usage of
T^{F}(t)
by Eq. (
) rather than
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
.
In terms of the order condition, we demand that
T^{F}(t)
becomes a
p
th-order PF of
e^{-iH^{F}t}
with the Floquet Hamiltonian
H^{F}=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}^{F}+(\Gamma-1)H^{\mathrm{LP}}
as shown in Eq. (
57
).
Associating
H_{0}
and
H_{\gamma}
respectively with
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
and
H_{\gamma}^{F}
, we arrive at the order condition of Eq. (
123
) for
H=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}+(\Gamma-1)H_{0}
.
In terms of the error bound, we observe commutators with
H_{\gamma}^{F}=H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}-H^{\mathrm{LP}}
instead of those with
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}
.
Remembering that
\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}}
and
\mathrm{ad}_{H^{\mathrm{LP}}}
respectively correspond to
\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\gamma}(t)}
and
-i\derivative{t}
in the original space, this is why the operator
\bar{\mathcal{D}}_{\gamma}(\tau)
is replaced by
\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}(t)=\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\gamma}(t)}+i\derivative{t}
for each
\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma
.

We also consider the standard PF
\bar{S}_{p}(t,0)
with the uniform coefficients as Definition
1
.
We can obtain a better error bound in that case as a corollary of Theorem
11
.
Since we have
\beta_{v\gamma}=\beta_{v}
, the coefficient
\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
in Eq. (
74
) for even
\tilde{\gamma}
is equal to
0
except for
\tilde{\gamma}=2\Gamma
.
Namely, the
\Gamma
copies of the derivative
\derivative{\tau}
included in the summation over even integers
\tilde{\gamma}
is replaced by a single copy.
This results in the following error bound in the uniform case.

Corollary 13
.

(Error of the uniform standard PF)

Let
\bar{S}_{p}(t,0)
be the
p
th-order standard PF having the uniform coefficients as Definition
1
.
Its error is bounded by

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}_p(t,0)}\leq 4(V_{p}t)^{p+1}\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau).

(125)

The quantity
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)
is defined by

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{p}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{p}=1}^{\Gamma+1}\sum_{\gamma_{0}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{\prod_{q=1}^p \mcl{\bar{D}}_{\gamma_q}^\mr{uni}(\tau) H_{\gamma_0}(\tau)},

(126)

where the operator
\mathcal{\bar{D}}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)
is given by
\mathcal{\bar{D}}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)=\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\gamma}(\tau)}
for
\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma
and
\mathcal{\bar{D}}_{\Gamma+1}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)=\derivative{\tau}
.

In contrast to Theorems
11
and
12
, the strength of the above error bound is directly independent of the number of terms
\Gamma
.
As discussed in Section
II.3
, it can be applied to the common examples of the standard PFs such as the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula and the Forest-Ruth-Suzuki formula.
The independence from
\Gamma
is important when considering long-range interactions, which will be discussed in Section
VI.3
.

Finally, we emphasize that better error bounds for time-independent PFs lead to better error bounds for time-dependent PFs.
We employ the time-independent PF error by Ref.
[
12
]
so that the error bounds for generic time-dependent PFs can be obtained, which are expected to be loose.
On the other hand, we can exploit any type of time-independent PF errors for evaluating
e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{T}^{F}(t)
or
e^{-iH^{F}t}-T^{F}(t)
in Section
V.1
.
If we know better time-independent PFs, we can improve the error bounds for the time-dependent PFs.
For instance, since a smaller coefficient of the error bounds is derived for the time-independent Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formulas
T_{p}(t)
up to
p=4

[
12
]
, the same goes also for the time-dependent cases.

V.4
Comparison with previous error bounds

There have been various attempts to obtain error bounds of time-dependent PFs so far, and we compare our results with them.
For low-order PFs, the explicit formulas for the errors have been known up to the second order
[
25
,
3
]
, as far as we know.
For instance, the first order Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula has an error bounded by Eq. (
24
).
Our result does not strictly reproduce it while they are qualitatively the same.
As we can see in Eq. (
112
), the former contains commutators of Hamiltonians at equal time, and the latter has those at different time.
Their error scaling in the system size coincides with each other owing to their commutator scaling in many of local Hamiltonians (see Section
VI
), although this difference brings the difference of their prefactors.

On the analysis of higher-order PF errors, Refs.
[
58
,
62
,
13
]
deal with the standard PFs for generic smooth time-dependent Hamiltonians.
Reference
[
62
]
proves an error bound called the
1
-norm scaling by Eqs. (
19
) and (
20
), which does not explicitly contain commutators.
Its system-size scaling for local Hamiltonians is worse than ours, which will be shown in Section
VI.2
.
Reference
[
13
]
shows an error bound whose scaling in the system size can decrease for local Hamiltonians with short-ranged Hamiltonians.
However, the explicit formula for the error is expressed by commutators involving many matrix exponentials
\exp(-i \alpha_{v\gamma} t\, \mr{ad}_{H_{\pi_v(\gamma)} (\beta_{v\gamma} t)})
in the middle.
Such a form makes it difficult to evaluate the system-size scaling when the interactions are long-ranged.
By contrast, our error bound is directly expressed by nested commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their derivatives.
It enables us to evaluate the system-size dependence of PF errors and to obtain its reduction for generic local Hamiltonians, as discussed in Section
VI
.

In terms of the way of derivation, we remark the approach based on time-shift operators
[
57
,
22
]
.
The time-shift operator is generated by the time derivative, which corresponds to
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
in the Floquet formalism, and it enables us to formally map the time-dependent PFs and MPFs to those for time-independent cases.
Approximating these relations with introducing an auxiliary system that labels the discretized time
[
60
]
, as we do for the Fourier index
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can reproduce the time-dependent PF or MPF errors by those in time-independent cases under the continuous time limit.
However, it is rather complicated to examine the convergence in that case compared to the Floquet formalism, in which the number of the discrete frequencies labeled by
\{\ket{l}\}_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}
goes to infinity instead.
Indeed, any error bound explicitly organized by commutators has not been shown by this approach for either the time-dependent PFs or MPFs.

VI
Computational cost with improved
N
-scaling

Commutator scaling plays a central role in reducing the computational cost with respect to the size
N
for local Hamiltonians.
In this section, we evaluate the cost for simulating time-dependent local Hamiltonians with the standard and generalized PFs.
While their errors by Theorem
11
, Theorem
12
, and Corollary
13
involve the time derivatives, we will show that time-dependent systems are simulated as efficiently as time-independent ones.

VI.1
Local time-dependent Hamiltonians

First of all, we define the locality and extensiveness for time-dependent Hamiltonians.
We say that
H(t)
is
k
-local if it is expressed by

H(t)=\sum_{X\subset\Lambda:|X|\leq k}h_{X}(t),

(127)

where each
h_{X}(t)
denotes at most
k
-body interactions acting on a domain
X\subset\Lambda
.
We consider local Hamiltonians having the locality
k\in\order{1}
.
When constructing the time-dependent PFs, we split
H(t)
into
\Gamma
terms by Eq. (
15
).
Each
H_{\gamma}(t)
can be also expanded by

H_{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{X\subset\Lambda:|X|\leq k}h_{X}^{\gamma}(t),

(128)

where the set of
\{h_{X}^{\gamma}(t)\}
satisfies
[h_{X}^{\gamma}(t),h_{X^{\prime}}^{\gamma}(t)]=0
and reproduces
h_{X}(t)
by
h_{X}(t)=\sum_{\gamma}h_{X}^{\gamma}(t)
.
The extensiveness
g
is defined by a number such that

\max_{i\in\Lambda}\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\sum_{X\subset\Lambda;X\ni i}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{h_X^\gamma(\tau)}\leq g

(129)

holds.
It means the maximal energy scale per site
[
31
]
, and depends on the range of interactions of the Hamiltonian.
We consider finite-range interactions (those working within finite distance), short-range interactions (those exponentially decaying in distance), and long-range interactions (those polynomially decaying in distance
r
as
r^{-\nu}
).
We can set the extensiveness
g
whose scaling is given by

g\in\begin{cases}\order{1}&(\text{finite- or short-range})\\
\order{1}&(\text{long-range, $\nu>d$})\\
\order{\log N}&(\text{long-range, $\nu=d$})\\
\order{N^{1-\frac{\nu}d}}&(\text{long-range, $\nu<d$})\end{cases},

(130)

where
d
denotes the spatial dimension of the lattice
\Lambda
.

As a characteristic quantity for time-dependent Hamiltonians, we additionally define the following value measuring the degree of time-dependency,
f
, by a number satisfying

\max_{i\in\Lambda}\sup_{\tau\in[0,t]}\sum_{X\subset\Lambda;X\ni i}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{\dv[q]{\tau} h_X^\gamma(\tau)}\leq f^{q}g

(131)

for every
q=1,2,\cdots,p
.
Each time derivative
H^{(q)}(t)
has the extensiveness smaller than
f^{q}g
by definition.
The
1
-norm factor
\alpha_{\text{1-norm},p}(\tau)
by Eq. (
20
) is bounded by

\alpha_{\text{1-norm},p}(\tau)\leq Ng\max\left[1,(f/g)^{\frac{p}{p+1}}\right].

(132)

For standard local Hamiltonians in condensed matter physics and quantum chemistry, where each local term
h_{X}^{\gamma}(t)
is driven locally, the strength of time-dependency
f
is usually size-independent.
For example, let us consider the case where each term varies in time as
h_{X}^{\gamma}(t)=h_{X}^{\gamma}\sin(\omega_X^\gamma t)
, which describes typical quantum systems subject to light with frequency
\omega_{X}^{\gamma}
.
The strength
f\in\order{\max_{X,\gamma} (\omega_X^\gamma)}
is usually a size-independent constant as long as we do not consider highly-oscillatory dynamics such that the frequency of the light
\omega_{X}^{\gamma}
is macroscopically large in the system size
N
.
We assume
f\in\order{N^0}
below considering standard local Hamiltonians in physics and chemistry.

VI.2
Bounds on time-dependent nested commutators

We next evaluate the upper bounds on
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
by Eqs. (
45
) and (
46
).
They are composed of nested commutators and their time derivatives, characterizing the error of the time-dependent PFs.
In order to conclude the substantial improvement by the commutator scaling, they should have scaling favorable with respect to the size
N
.
Based on the locality and the extensiveness, we obtain the following bounds.

Theorem 14
.

Local and extensive Hamiltonians
H(t)
satisfy the inequalities,

\displaystyle\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p!(2kg+\Gamma f)^{p}Ng,

(133)

\displaystyle\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p!(2kg+2\Gamma f)^{p}Ng,

(134)

\displaystyle\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p!(2kg+f)^{p}Ng,

(135)

where the commutator factors
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
,
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
, and
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)
are defined respectively by Eqs. (
45
), (
46
), and (
126
).

Proof.—
We focus on the quantity
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
.
We first evaluate the upper bound on

\Gamma^{p-r}\sum_{\gamma_{0},\cdots,\gamma_{r}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{\left[ \prod_{r'=1}^r \ad_{H_{\gamma_{r'}}^{(q_{r'})}(\tau)}\right] H_{\gamma_0}^{(q_0)}(\tau)}

(136)

for
r,q_{0},\cdots,q_{r}
such that
r+q_{0}+\cdots+q_{r}=p
.
The integer
p-r
mean the number of times that the derivative
\bar{D}_{\Gamma+1}(\tau)=\Gamma\derivative{\tau}
is chosen in
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
.
We decompose each
H^{(q_{r^{\prime}})}_{\gamma_{r^{\prime}}}
into local terms by

H^{(q_{r^{\prime}})}_{\gamma_{r^{\prime}}}(\tau)=\sum_{X_{r^{\prime}}\subset\Lambda:|X_{r^{\prime}}|\leq k}h_{X_{r^{\prime}}}^{\gamma_{r^{\prime}}(q_{r^{\prime}})}(\tau).

(137)

Let us define the union
Y_{r^{\prime}}=X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{r^{\prime}-1}
for
r^{\prime}\geq 2
.
The nested commutator among the local terms
\{h_{X_{r^{\prime}}}^{\gamma_{r^{\prime}}(q_{r^{\prime}})}\}
vanishes if there exists an index
r^{\prime}
such that
X_{r^{\prime}}\cap Y_{r^{\prime}}=\phi
.
As a result, with repeating the relation
\norm{[A,B]}\leq 2\norm{A}\norm{B}
, we obtain

\displaystyle[\text{Eq. (\ref{Eq_Cost:p_com_r_deriv})}]

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2^{r}\Gamma^{p-r}\sum_{\gamma_{0},\cdots,\gamma_{r}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{0},\cdots,X_{r}:\\
X_{r^{\prime}}\cap Y_{r^{\prime}}\neq\phi\end{subarray}}\prod_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r}\norm{h_{X_{r'}}^{\gamma_{r'} (q_{r'})}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2^{r}\Gamma^{p-r}\sum_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{X_{0}}\norm{h_{X_0}^{\gamma_0 (q_0)}(\tau)}\prod_{r^{\prime}=1}^{r}\left(r^{\prime}kf^{q_{r^{\prime}}}g\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leq r!(2kg)^{r}(\Gamma f)^{p-r}Ng.

(138)

The second inequality comes from the fact that each
h_{X_{r^{\prime}}}^{\gamma_{r^{\prime}}(q_{r^{\prime}})}(\tau)
has the locality
k
and the extensiveness
f^{q_{r^{\prime}}}g
by Eq. (
131
), which leads to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{r^{\prime}}:|X_{r^{\prime}}|\leq k,\\
X_{r^{\prime}}\cap Y_{r^{\prime}}\neq\phi\end{subarray}}\norm{h_{X_{r'}}^{\gamma_{r'} (q_{r'})}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in Y_{r^{\prime}}}\sum_{X_{r^{\prime}}:X_{r^{\prime}}\ni i}\norm{h_{X_{r'}}^{\gamma_{r'} (q_{r'})}(\tau)}

(139)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle r^{\prime}kf^{q_{r^{\prime}}}g.

We use the relation
|Y_{r^{\prime}}|=|X_{0}\cup\cdots\cup X_{r^{\prime}-1}|\leq r^{\prime}k
in the second inequality.

The quantity
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
can be decomposed into terms having the form of Eq. (
136
).
Let
Q
denote a possible subset of
(1,\cdots,p)
such that
\gamma_{q^{\prime}}=\Gamma+1
for
q^{\prime}\in Q
.
This leads to the expression of
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
by

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{r=0}^{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subset(1,\cdots,p):\\
|Q|=p-r\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\sum_{\gamma_{0},\cdots,\gamma_{q}=1}^{\Gamma}}_{\text{$q^{\prime}\notin Q$}}\norm{ \left[ \prod_{q=1}^p \underbrace{\bar{\mcl{D}}_{\gamma_{q'}}(\tau)}_{\text{$\Gamma\dv{\tau}$ if $q' \in Q$}}\right] H_{\gamma_0}(\tau)}.

Considering the Leibnitz rule, the above equation contains at most
(r+1)^{p-r}
copies of Eq. (
136
) for each
r
and
Q
such that
|Q|=p-r
.
As a result, we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subset(1,\cdots,p):\\
|Q|=p-r\end{subarray}}(r+1)^{p-r}r!(2kg)^{r}(\Gamma f)^{p-r}Ng

(141)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p!Ng\sum_{r=0}^{p}\frac{p!}{(p-r)!r!}(2kg)^{r}(\Gamma f)^{p-r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p!(2kg+\Gamma f)^{p}Ng.

This completes the proof of Eq. (
133
).

The inequalities for
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
and
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)
are obtained in a similar manner.
Using the triangle inequality, the quantity
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
defined by Eq. (
46
) is further bounded by

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{v_{1},\cdots,v_{p}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{p}=1}^{\Gamma+1}\sum_{\gamma_{0}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{\prod_{q=1}^p \mcl{D}_{\gamma_q}'(\tau) H_{\gamma_0}(\tau)},

with
\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\gamma}(\tau)}
for
\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma
and
\mathcal{D}_{\Gamma+1}^{\prime}(\tau)=2\Gamma\derivative{\tau}
.
Thus, Eq. (
134
) is obtained by replacing
\Gamma f
in Eq. (
133
) by
2\Gamma f
.
Similarly, replacing
\Gamma f
by
f
results in Eq. (
135
).
\quad\square

These bounds reproduce the bound on the nested commutators for time-independent cases, Eq. (
13
)
[
12
]
,

\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}\leq p!(2kg)^{p}Ng,

(143)

at
f=0
.
Errors of the time-dependent PFs for small time
t
are bounded scale as

\displaystyle\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\order{(gt+\Gamma ft)^p Ngt},

(144)

\displaystyle\norm{U(t,0)-S(t,0)}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\order{(gt+\Gamma ft)^p Ngt},

(145)

for orders
p\in\order{1}
.
When the standard PF is uniform, the error scaling becomes

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}_p(t,0)}\in\order{(gt+ft)^p Ngt}.

(146)

They are substantially improved from the previous result hosting the
1
-norm scaling
[
62
]
,

\displaystyle\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}_p(t,0)}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\order{\left( \max_{\tau\in[0,t]} \alpha_{\text{1-norm},p} (\tau) t\right)^{p+1}}

(147)

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\order{(Ngt+Nft)^p Ngt},

with respect to the size
N
[See Eq. (
19
)].

VI.3
Gate counts for time-dependent PFs

We are ready for evaluating the computational cost.
For simulating sufficiently large evolution time
t
, we split it into
r
parts and approximate the time evolution
U(t,0)
by the product of time-dependent PFs for small time
t/r
.
Namely, the number
r
, often called the Trotter number, is the query complexity in PFs.
The Trotter number
r
is determined so that the desirable error
\varepsilon
can be achieved.
For instance, the requirement when using the standard PF is

\displaystyle\norm{ U(t,0)-\prod_{l=1}^r \bar{S}(lt/r,(l-1)t/r)}\leq\varepsilon.

(148)

Since the left-hand side is bounded by

\displaystyle\norm{ U(t,0)-\prod_{l=1}^r \bar{S}(lt/r,(l-1)t/r)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{l=1}^{r}\norm{U(lt/r,(l-1)t/r)-\bar{S}_p(lt/r,(l-1)t/r)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 4\left(\frac{V_{p}t}{r}\right)^{p+1}\sum_{l=1}^{r}\max_{\tau\in[(l-1)t/r,lt/r]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{4(2V_{p}kgt+V_{p}\Gamma ft)^{p}V_{p}Ngt}{r^{p}}

(149)

by Theorem
11
and Theorem
14
, it is sufficient to choose
r
by

r=\left\lceil V_{p}(2kg+\Gamma f)t\left(\frac{4V_{p}Ngt}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\rceil.

(150)

Similar analysis applies to the generalized PF and the standard PF in the uniform case.
The scaling of the Trotter number
r
is summarized as follows,

r\in\begin{cases}\displaystyle\Theta\left((g+\Gamma f)t\left(\frac{Ngt}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)&\text{for $\bar{S}(t,0)$, $S(t,0)$}\\
\displaystyle\Theta\left((g+f)t\left(\frac{Ngt}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)&\text{for $\bar{S}_{p}(t,0)$}\end{cases}.

(151)

We estimate the gate counts for Hamiltonian simulation.
Here, the cost is measured by the number of
\order{1}
-local quantum gates.
We do not assume the geometrical locality, i.e.,
\order{1}
-local gates between distant qubits are available with the same cost as geometrically-local gates.
In the time-dependent PFs, each local time evolution under
h_{X}^{\gamma}(t)
can be implemented by
\order{1}

k
-qubit quantum gates.
We note that, even though we use the time-ordered product for the generalized PF, the local time evolution given by

\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-i\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau h_{X}^{\gamma}(\tau)\right)

(152)

is classically computable, and hence it can be decomposed into local gates.
The gate count per query to the time-dependent PFs is proportional to the number of local terms in the Hamiltonian
H(t)
.
As a result, the total gate count amounts to
\order{Nr}
for finite- and short-ranged interacting systems and
\order{N^kr}
for
k
-local long-ranged interacting systems.
We summarize these results in Table
1
.

We remark the
\Gamma
-dependency of the cost, which is present for the standard and the generalized PFs in general but absent for the standard PF with uniform coefficients.
The number of terms in PFs,
\Gamma
, in Eq. (
15
) depends on the range of interactions.
The number
\Gamma
can be
\order{1}
for generic Hamiltonians with finite-range interactions.
For instance, we can decompose a 1d Hamiltonian with nearest-neighbor interactions
H(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}h_{i,i+1}(t)
into
H(t)=H_{1}(t)+H_{2}(t)
with
H_{1}(t)=\sum_{i:\text{odd}}h_{i,i+1}(t)
and
H_{2}(t)=\sum_{i:\text{even}}h_{i,i+1}(t)
.
The time-evolution operators under
H_{1}(t)
or
H_{2}(t)
can be implemented by
\order{N}
local quantum gates.
In general, we can decompose the set of finite-range interactions into
\Gamma\in\order{1}
groups so that each of them is composed of mutually-commuting terms, and we can perform such an implementation.
For short-ranged interacting systems,
\Gamma
can be regarded as
\order{1}
since the exponentially small interactions can be truncated by Lieb-Robinson bound
[
33
]
.
Therefore, the number
\Gamma
is irrelevant to the cost for Hamiltonians with finite- and short-range interactions.

On the other hand, the quantity
\Gamma
becomes significant for long-ranged interacting systems.
Let us consider the
2
-local case, whose Hamiltonian can be generally written by

H(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in\Lambda\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}=1}^{3}h_{ij}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(t)P_{i}^{\sigma}P_{j}^{\sigma^{\prime}}+\sum_{i\in\Lambda}\sum_{\sigma=1}^{3}h_{i}^{\sigma}(t)P_{i}^{\sigma}

(153)

with some coefficients
h_{ij}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(t),h_{i}^{\sigma}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
.
The symbol
P_{i}^{\sigma}
denotes the Pauli operators
X,Y,Z
at the site
i
respectively for
\sigma=1,2,3
.
The decomposition
H(t)=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}(t)
such that each local term in
H_{\gamma}(t)
commutes with one another can be organized by a divide-and-conquer method
[
38
]
.
Figure
1
shows the decomposition of the two-body interactions when the spatial dimension
d
is
1
.
At the
\gamma^{\prime}
-th stage for
\gamma^{\prime}=1,\cdots,\lceil\log_{2}N\rceil
, we split the lattice
\Lambda
into
2^{\gamma^{\prime}}
blocks with the size
N/2^{\gamma^{\prime}}
.
We consider pairwise interactions between adjacent blocks
L_{\mu}^{\gamma^{\prime}}
and
R_{\mu}^{\gamma^{\prime}}
separately for each
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}=1,2,3
.
Namely, we choose

H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(t)=\sum_{\mu=1}^{2^{\gamma^{\prime}-1}}\sum_{i\in L_{\mu}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}\sum_{j\in R_{\mu}^{\gamma^{\prime}}}h_{ij}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(t)P_{i}^{\sigma}P_{j}^{\sigma^{\prime}}

(154)

as each term
H_{\gamma}(t)
with the index
\gamma=(\gamma^{\prime},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
.
We set the last term by
H_{\Gamma}(t)=\sum_{i}\sum_{\sigma}h_{i}^{\sigma}(t)P_{i}^{\sigma}
.
The number of terms amounts to
\Gamma=9\lceil\log_{2}N\rceil+1\in\order{\log N}
.
Based on the same strategy, the partition number
\Gamma
can be
\order{N^{k-1} \log N}
for Hamiltonians with
k
-local long-range interactions on a
d
-dimensional lattice.
Therefore, the standard and generalized PFs are generally efficient for Hamiltonians with
2
-local long-range interactions.
On the other hand, the standard PF with uniform coefficients, which is common with the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter or Forest-Ruth-Suzuki formulas, is applicable to those with generic
k
-local long-range interactions owing to the
\Gamma
-independence of the cost.

Figure 1:
Decomposition of a Hamiltonian
H(t)=\sum_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}(t)
when the interactions are long-ranged. This decomposition is based on Ref.
[
38
]
and can be generalized to generic dimension
d
.

VI.4
Comparison with the previous results

We first compare our gate count estimates with the previously obtained ones for the time-dependent PFs.
The cost of the standard PF based on the
1
-norm scaling error, Eq. (
19
), is shown in the first row of Table
1

[
62
]
, which mainly focused on the Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula.
Our result for the standard PF with uniform coefficients completely overwhelms it with respect to the system size
N
, giving the
N
times smaller cost.
This improvement comes from the substantial error reduction from Eq. (
147
) to Eq. (
146
) based on the error bound with the commutator scaling, in which the locality of the Hamiltonian plays a central role.
Our results for the standard and generalized PFs in generic forms are also superior to the one by the
1
-norm scaling error for Hamiltonians with finite-, short-, and
2
-local long-range interactions.
Ref.
[
13
]
has recently derived the cost of the standard PF for a generic order
p\in\mathbb{N}
based on the commutator-scaling error.
Our result reproduces its result for finite- and short-ranged interactions.
The advantage of our formalism lies in the applicability to generic local Hamiltonians.
Our error bound is expressed directly by nested commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}_{\gamma}
and their time derivative, thereby allowing us to obtain its scaling as Eqs. (
144
)-(
146
).
We arrive at the size-efficient cost brought by the commutator scaling also for Hamiltonians with long-range interactions, while it is not achieved in Ref.
[
13
]
.

We also compare the results with linear combination of unitaries (LCU)
[
7
]
and quantum singular-value transformation (QSVT)
[
16
]
, known as promising post-Trotter algorithms.
These algorithms have exponentially better dependence in the inverse error
1/\varepsilon
than PFs.
While they were originally invented for time-independent systems, they have been partially extended for time-dependent cases, such as LCU for generic time-dependent Hamiltonians
[
39
,
28
]
and QSVT for time-periodic and -quasiperiodic Hamiltonians
[
40
,
42
]
.
Their costs are shown in Table
1

While the post-Trotter algorithms keep the exponential advantage in the inverse error
1/\varepsilon
, our results show that the time-dependent PFs have better scaling in the system size
N
for generic local Hamiltonians with finite-, short-, and long-range interactions.
Such an advantage in the system size has already been understood for time-independent PFs due to their commutator-scaling errors
[
12
]
.
By contrast, it has been unclear for time-dependent cases since the conventional analysis based on the
1
-norm scaling error cannot predict it.
Our analysis has shown that the time-dependent PFs are superior to the post-Trotter algorithms, LCU and QSVT, with respect to the system size for a generic order
p
and Hamiltonians with generic local interactions.

VII
Time-dependent multi-product formula

A multi-product formula (MPF) is a linear combination of PFs
[
14
]
, and a promising algorithm that can have both the efficiency in the system size like PF and the one in the accuracy like LCU.
For time-independent cases, a
p
th-based order-
m
MPF
M(t)
is defined by a linear combination of the
p
th-order PF
T(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
,

M(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}[T(t/k_{j})]^{k_{j}},

(155)

satisfying the order condition
M(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{m+1}}
.
The set of coefficients
\{c_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\}_{j=1}^{J}
and the set of natural numbers
\{k_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\}_{j=1}^{J}
with
J\in\mathbb{N}
are determined by the order condition.
With a particular choice of
\{c_{j}\}_{j}
and
\{k_{j}\}_{j}
, called the well-conditioned solution, MPF can exponentially improve the gate complexity in the allowable error compared to the PF for time-independent Hamiltonians
[
37
,
1
,
43
]
.

In contrast, MPFs for time-dependent Hamiltonians are poorly understood.
Ref.
[
60
]
has provided the construction of time-dependent MPFs, and obtained their error bound and cost.
Although they imply the existence of the commutator scaling for time-dependent cases, any formula explicitly expressed by commutators has not been derived yet.
Thus, it still remains an open problem whether the time-dependent MPFs can simulate time-dependent systems exponentially faster than the time-dependent PFs with respect to the inverse error
1/\varepsilon
while keeping the system-size efficiency brought by the commutator scaling.

In this section, we give an error bound having the commutator scaling for the time-dependent MPF in a similar strategy for the time-dependent PFs.
We clarify the relationship between the time-independent and -dependent MPFs in Section
VII.1
.
We give the error bound by evaluating the one for the time-independent case with infinite-dimension in Section
VII.2
, while its detailed derivation is provided in Appendix
C
.
Section
VII.3
discusses the computational cost of the time-dependent MPF.

VII.1
Relation between time-independent and time-dependent MPFs

We begin by introducing time-dependent MPFs and show that they have time-independent counterparts on the infinite-dimensional Floquet-Hilbert space as well as the PFs.
As a natural extension of Eq. (
155
), we define the standard and generalized MPFs for time-dependent Hamiltonians.

Definition 15
.

(Time-dependent MPFs)

The
p
th-based order-
m
standard and generalized MPFs
\bar{N}(t,0)
and
N(t,0)
for time-dependent Hamiltonians are defined respectively by

\displaystyle\bar{N}(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}\prod_{l=1}^{k_{j}}\bar{S}\left(\frac{lt}{k_{j}},\frac{(l-1)t}{k_{j}}\right),

(156)

\displaystyle N(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}\prod_{l=1}^{k_{j}}S\left(\frac{lt}{k_{j}},\frac{(l-1)t}{k_{j}}\right),

(157)

satisfying the order condition,

\displaystyle\bar{N}(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U(t,0)+\order{t^{m+1}},

(158)

\displaystyle N(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U(t,0)+\order{t^{m+1}}.

(159)

The operators
\bar{S}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
S(\cdot,\cdot)
are the
p
th-order standard and generalized PFs by Eqs. (
16
) and (
22
).
We denote the standard MPF constructed by the standard PFs in the uniform case
\bar{S}_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
by
\bar{N}_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

As we will discuss later, the set of the coefficients
\{c_{j}\}
and the integers
\{k_{j}\}
is common with the time-independent cases in Eq. (
155
).
The time-dependent MPFs can be implemented on quantum circuits by the LCU method
[
7
]
.
We evaluate their error bounds for proving their efficiency.
The strategy is the same as the one for the time-dependent PFs in Section
IV
.
First, we show the correspondence between the time-dependent and -independent MPFs as follows.

Theorem 16
.

(Relation of MPFs)

Let
\bar{M}^{F}(t)
and
M^{F}(t)
be time-independent MPFs on the Floquet-Hilbert space, defined by

\displaystyle\bar{M}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}[\bar{T}^{F}(t/k_{j})]^{k_{j}},

(160)

\displaystyle M^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J}c_{j}[T^{F}(t/k_{j})]^{k_{j}}.

(161)

The operators
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
and
T^{F}(t)
are the
p
th-order time-independent PFs for the standard and generalized PFs by Eqs. (
49
) and (
).
The time-dependent MPFs are expressed by

\displaystyle\bar{N}(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|\bar{M}^{F}(t)|0},

(162)

\displaystyle N(t,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|M^{F}(t)|0}.

(163)

Proof.—

The proof is similar to the one for Theorem
4
.
We focus on the standard MPF
\bar{M}(t,0)
.
Using the translation symmetry of the Floquet Hamiltonian
H^{F}
[See Eq. (
36
)] and the absolute convergence, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\bra{l}[\bar{T}^{F}(t/k_{j})]^{k_{j}}\ket{0}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{1},\cdots,l_{k_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
l_{0}=0\end{subarray}}e^{-il_{k_{j}}\omega t}\prod_{k=1}^{k_{j}}\braket{l_{k}|\bar{T}^{F}(t/k_{j})|l_{k-1}}

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{k=1}^{k_{j}}\sum_{l_{1},\cdots,l_{k_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il_{k}\omega(k/k_{j})t}\braket{l_{k}|\bar{T}^{F}(t/k_{j})|0}

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{k=1}^{k_{j}}\bar{S}(kt/k_{j},(k-1)t/k_{j}).

(164)

We use Theorem
4
in the last equality.
We obtain Eq. (
162
) by taking the summation with the weight
c_{j}
.
The proof for the generalized MPF is parallel to this discussion.
\quad\square

This theorem states that the errors of the time-dependent MPFs can be evaluated via those for time-independent MPFs like

U(t,0)-\bar{N}(t,0)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|\left[e^{-iH^{F}t}-\bar{M}^{F}(t)\right]|0}.

(165)

Not only it allows the error analysis of time-independent MPF for time-dependent one as well as PF, but also it says that the coefficients
\{c_{j}\}
and
\{k_{j}\}
constructed for time-independent cases qualify as those for time-dependent cases.
Namely, using the coefficients for the well-conditioned MPF
[
37
]
, the time-dependent MPFs can be efficiently implemented by LCU with a small overhead.

VII.2
Error bounds of time-dependent MPF

We discuss the error bounds of the time-dependent MPFs while the detailed derivation is given in Appendix
C
.
We suppose that
H(t)=\sum_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}(t)
is a smooth Hamiltonian such that each
H_{\gamma}(t)
belongs to class
C^{\infty}
here.
This is in contrast to the time-dependent PFs, where class
C^{p+2}
is sufficient for the order
p
as discussed in Section
III.1
.
The Hamiltonian
H(t)
is supposed to be
k
-local and
g
-extensive [See Eq. (
129
)].
The degree of the local time-dependency
f
is defined by a number satisfying Eq. (
131
) for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
, reflecting the change in differentiability.
We prove the following bound on the standard MPF error.

Theorem 17
.

(Error of the standard MPF)

Let
\epsilon
be an arbitrary fixed value in
(0,1)
.
We define a quantity expressed by nested commutators,

\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathbb{N}:\\
p\leq q\leq p_{0}(N,\epsilon)\end{subarray}}\left(\left[\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},q}(\tau)\right]^{\frac{1}{q+1}}\right),

(166)

where
p_{0}(N,\epsilon)
and
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},q}(\tau)
are respectively defined by
p_{0}(N,\epsilon)=\lceil\log(2N/\epsilon)\rceil
and Eq. (
45
).
When the time
t
is small enough to satisfy

t\in\order{\min\left( \frac{1}{p_0(N,\epsilon)(g+\Gamma f)}, \frac{1}{\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mr{com},p}^\mr{MPF} (\tau)}\right)},

(167)

the error of the standard MPF is bounded by

\displaystyle\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{N}(t,0)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}\left(2V_{p}\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)t\right)^{m+1}+\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}\|\vec{k}\|_{1}\epsilon.

(168)

When simulating large evolution time
t
, the requirement Eq. (
167
) should be satisfied for each Trotter step
t/r
.
Since the Trotter number
r
for the MPF will be large enough for it, this requirement does not matter in practice.
We also note that there is an option of
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
For achieving the desirable error
\varepsilon
, it will be chosen as
\epsilon\in o(\varepsilon)
.
While we provide the proof of this theorem in Appendix
C
, it is essentially parallel to the time-dependent PFs in Section
V
.
The error of the time-dependent MPFs can be expressed by a time-independent counterpart by Eq. (
165
) owing to Theorem
16
.
Then, we apply the analysis for the time-independent MPF
\bar{M}^{F}(t)

[
1
,
43
]
.

We next identify the scaling of
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)
, which plays a central role in characterizing the commutator scaling of the standard MPF.
For generic local Hamiltonians, we obtain the following upper bound.

Lemma 18
.

The quantity
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)
defined by Eq. (
166
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2p[(2kg+\Gamma f)^{p}gN]^{\frac{1}{p+1}}+2e(2kg+\Gamma f)p_{0}(N,\epsilon)

\displaystyle\quad\in\order{ [(g+\Gamma f)^p g N]^{\frac 1{p+1}} + (g+\Gamma f)\log(N/\epsilon) }.

(169)

Proof.—

Using the upper bound of
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},q}(\tau)
by Theorem
14
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},q}(\tau)\right]^{\frac{1}{q+1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle[q!(2kg+\Gamma f)^{q}Ng]^{\frac{1}{q+1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(q+1)\left(\frac{Ng}{2kg+\Gamma f}\right)^{\frac{1}{q+1}}(2kg+\Gamma f).

Since the function
xa^{\frac{1}{x}}
(
a>0
: a constant) monotonically decreases in
x<\log a
and increases in
\log a<x
, the right-hand side becomes the largest at either
q=p
or
q=p_{0}(N,\epsilon)
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)}{2kg+\Gamma f}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(p+1)\left(\frac{Ng}{2kg+\Gamma f}\right)^{\frac{1}{p+1}}

\displaystyle\quad+[p_{0}(N,\epsilon)+1]\left(\frac{Ng}{2kg+\Gamma f}\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{0}(N,\epsilon)+1}}.

By using the relation,
N^{1/p_{0}(N,\epsilon)}\leq e^{\log N/\log(3N/\epsilon)}<e
, we obtain Eq. (
169
).
\quad\square

The error bounds for the generalized MPF
N(t,0)
and the standard one in the uniform case
\bar{N}_{p}(t,0)
are obtained in the same way.
The quantity
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)
in the error bound Eq. (
168
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{MPF}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathbb{N}:\\
p\leq q\leq p_{0}(N,\epsilon)\end{subarray}}\left(\left[\alpha_{\mathrm{com},q}(\tau)\right]^{\frac{1}{q+1}}\right),

(172)

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni,MPF}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathbb{N}:\\
p\leq q\leq p_{0}(N,\epsilon)\end{subarray}}\left(\left[\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},q}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)\right]^{\frac{1}{q+1}}\right),

(173)

respectively for
N(t,0)
and
\bar{N}_{p}(t,0)
, in which
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},q}(\tau)
and
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},q}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)
are defined by Eqs. (
46
) and (
126
).
As well as the discussion for PFs in Section
VI
, their scalings can be evaluated by substituting
2\Gamma f
[for
N(t,0)
] or
f
[for
\bar{N}_{p}(t,0)
] into
\Gamma f
.
We note that this replacement also applies to the requirement on the time, Eq. (
167
).

Ref.
[
60
]
has recently shown the error bound of the standard MPF in the uniform case
\bar{N}_{p}(t,0)
, especially composed of the second order Lie-Suzuki-Trotter formula.
It obeys the
1
-norm scaling, which amounts to

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{N}_p(t,0)}\in\order{\left[ (Ngt+Nft)^p Ngt\right]^{\frac{m+1}{p+1}}}.

(174)

By contrast, combining Theorem
17
and Lemma
18
for the uniform case results in the following system-size dependence,

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{N}_p(t,0)}\in\order{\left[ (gt+ft)^p Ngt\right]^{\frac{m+1}{p+1}}},

(175)

where we neglect logarithmic terms.
It is polynomially better than the
1
-norm scaling.
In particular, while increasing the order
p
does not improve the system-size dependence of the
1
-norm scaling, Eq. (
174
), the commutator-scaling error by Eq. (
175
) decreases.

VII.3
Cost of time-dependent MPFs

We evaluate the cost of Hamiltonian simulation by using the time-dependent MPFs for local Hamiltonians.
The algorithm runs in a similar manner to the one with the time-independent MPFs as follows
[
37
]
.
We focus on the standard MPF.
First, we split the large evolution time
t
into
r
parts like PF.
The Trotter number
r
is determined so that the
r
-times repetition of the MPF for the small time
t/r
approximates the time evolution within a desirable error as

\norm{U(t,0)- \prod_{r'=0}^{r-1} \bar{N}\left(\frac{(r^\prime+1)t}{r},\frac{r't}r\right)}\in\order{\varepsilon}.

(176)

It is sufficient to make the error at each step bounded by

\norm{U\left(\frac{(r'+1)t}{r},\frac{r't}{r}\right)-\bar{N}\left(\frac{(r'+1)t}{r},\frac{r't}{r}\right)}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2r},

(177)

for every
r^{\prime}=1,\cdots,r
.
Each MPF for the time
t/r
is implemented by LCU
[
7
]
, where we use the controlled operation of the time-dependent PF,

C[\bar{S}(t_{2},t_{1})]=\ket{0}\bra{0}\otimes I+\ket{1}\bra{1}\otimes\bar{S}(t_{2},t_{1}),

(178)

with certain

t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
The number of queries to
C[\bar{S}(t_{2},t_{1})]
amounts to

\order{r \norm{\vec{c}}_1 \| \vec{k} \|_1}.

(179)

The factor
\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}
represents the cost of quantum amplitude amplification
[
10
]
, which is required to implemente the LCU deterministically.
The last factor
\|\vec{k}\|_{1}
is the number of queries for each time evolution in the linear combination.
The number of ancilla qubits for implementing the LCU is
\lceil\log_{2}J\rceil
.

We evaluate the computational cost with determining the Trotter number
r
, the order
m
, and the number of terms
J
.
We set the value
\epsilon\in(0,1)
by
\epsilon=\varepsilon/4\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}\|\vec{k}\|_{1}r
in Theorem
17
.
Equation (
177
) suggests that the scaling of the proper Trotter number
r
can be obtained by solving

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}\frac{[(gt+\Gamma ft)^{p}Ngt]^{\frac{m+1}{p+1}}}{r^{m}}\in\order{\varepsilon},\\
\displaystyle\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}\frac{[(gt+\Gamma ft)\log\,(N\norm{\vec{c}}_{1}\|\vec{k}\|_{1}r/\varepsilon)]^{m+1}}{r^{m}}\in\order{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

(180)

These inequalities are the essentially the same as those required in the time-independent MPF (See Theorem 6 in Ref.
[
43
]
), where
N^{\frac{1}{p+1}}
and
g
are replaced respectively by
\{Ng(g+\Gamma f)^{-1}\}^{\frac{1}{p+1}}
and
g+\Gamma f
, reflecting the time-dependency.
We set the order
m
by

m=\left\lceil\log\left(\frac{N(g+\Gamma f)t}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\rceil.

(181)

When the coefficients
\{c_{j}\}
and the integers
\{k_{j}\}
are organized as the well-conditioned MPF
[
37
]
, the number
J
is proportional to
m
and the coefficients scale as

\norm{\vec{c}}_{1},\|\vec{k}\|_{1}\in\mathrm{poly}\left(J\right)\subset\mathrm{polylog}(N,gt,\Gamma ft,1/\varepsilon).

(182)

Then, there exists a Trotter number such that

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\Theta\biggl([(g+\Gamma f)^{p}gN]^{\frac{1}{p+1}}t\biggr.

(183)

\displaystyle\qquad\biggl.+(g+\Gamma f)t\log^{2}\left(\frac{N(g+\Gamma f)t}{\varepsilon}\right)\biggr).

The second term except for
\Gamma ft
in the above equation can be absorbed into the first term or the poly-logarithmic factors in the symbol
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\cdot\right)
.
Consequently, the query complexity in the controlled standard PF
C[\bar{S}(t_{2},t_{1})]
amounts to

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left([(g+\Gamma f)^{p}gN]^{\frac{1}{p+1}}t+\Gamma ft\right).

(184)

The number of
\order{1}
-qubit gates is obtained by multiplying the query complexity with the number of gates for the controlled PF, which is proportional to the number of local interactions in the Hamiltonian.
The number of ancilla qubits is determined by
\order{\log J}
with the scaling of
J\propto m
by Eq. (
181
).
The computational resources for the generalized MPF
S(t,0)
or the standard MPF in the uniform case
\bar{S}_{p}(t,0)
are obtained similarly, in which we replace
\Gamma f
in Eq. (
184
) by
2\Gamma f
or
f
.
We summarize these results in Table
1
.
As well as PFs, the difference among the time-dependent MPFs appear in the applicability to Hamiltonians with long-range interactions due to the number
\Gamma
in their cost.
The standard MPF in the uniform case
\bar{N}_{p}(t,0)
, which is the most commonly used, can be employed for generic local Hamiltonians.
On the other hand, the standard MPF
\bar{N}(t,0)
and the generalized MPF
N(t,0)
are efficient for Hamiltonians with finite- or short-range interactions and some classes of long-range interactions such that the number of partitions
\Gamma
can be small (See Section
VI
).

The time-dependent MPFs achieve the polylogarithmic dependency in
1/\varepsilon
, overcoming the shortcoming of the PFs.
While such efficiency in the allowable error has already been predicted by the
1
-norm scaling
[
60
]
, we prove that the time-dependent MPFs are efficient also in the system size.
To be precise, when we focus on the system-size dependence, their gate counts achieve the scaling
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(N^{1+1/(p+1)}\right)
(for finite-range interactions) or
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(N^{k+1/(p+1)}g\right)
(for
k
-local long-range interactions).
These are as large as those for the
p
th-order time-dependent PFs obtained based on the commutator-scaling errors in Section
VI
, and smaller than those for the post-Trotter algorithms like LCU and QSVT.
This fact gives an evidence of the commutator scaling in the time-dependent MPFs.
To conclude, the time-dependent MPFs are simultaneously efficient in the system size, the evolution time, and the inverse error like the time-independent MPFs
[
43
]
.
We note that MPFs require distant local gates and some ancilla qubits in contrast to PFs.

We make comparison also with the Haah-Hastings-Kothari-Low (HHKL) algorithm
[
20
]
, which can be efficient in
N,t,1/\varepsilon
like MPF.
This algorithm achieves the near-optimal gate count
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(Nt\right)
for Hamiltonians with finite- or short-range interactions.
The time-dependent MPFs have a little worse scaling in the system size
N
.
However, the HHKL algorithm strongly relies on the Lieb-Robinson bound
[
33
]
and cannot keep its efficiency in the inverse error
1/\varepsilon
for long-range interactions
[
59
]
.
Since the time-dependent MPFs only exploit the locality via the commutator scaling, it serves as a versatile method for generic local Hamiltonians as well as time-independent cases.

VIII
Conclusion and Discussion

In this paper, we derive the explicit error bounds for the product and multi-product formulas (PFs and MPFs) for generic smooth time-dependent Hamiltonians
H(t)=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}(t)
.
The error bounds are expressed by the commutators among
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their time derivatives, which decreases the cost of time-dependent Hamiltonian simulation in the system size.
While the additional error term arising from the time derivatives are inherent in time-dependent cases, we show that various time-dependent local Hamiltonians can be simulated as efficiently as time-independent cases with exploiting this commutator scaling.
The derivation of the error bound itself is insightful.
We use Floquet theory and discover that generic time-dependent PFs are mapped to time-independent one defined on an infinite-dimensional space.
This enables us to evaluate the error bound by the technique for the time-independent PFs, although we have to confirm the convergence of the summation over the infinite-dimensional space for getting a meaningful error bound.
By the careful calculation based on the translation symmetry appearing in Floquet theory, we obtain the error bound explicitly expressed by the commutators of
\{H_{\gamma}(t)\}
and their derivatives.

We leave some future directions.
Our results will lead to the acceleration of various quantum algorithms relying on time-dependent PFs, such as time-independent Hamiltonian simulation exploiting an interaction picture
[
39
,
9
,
51
]
.
Since the interaction picture under integrable Hamiltonians maintains the locality and the range of interactions, the commutator scaling will improve the PF error and thereby the cost of Hamiltonian simulation.
Recently, the error analysis on PFs has diverged to various directions, such as the low-energy subspace simulation
[
50
,
17
,
23
,
41
]
and the connections to thermalization
[
24
,
52
]
, and their extensions to time-dependent Hamiltonians will be interesting.
In particular, concerning to the former example, the time-independent PFs have been proven to host substantial decrease in their errors when the initial states belong to low-energy subspaces
[
50
]
, and the commutator scaling plays a significant role there
[
23
,
41
]
.
If one can find the time-dependent analogue based on our formalism, it will accelerate quantum algorithms for adiabatic state preparation
[
63
,
4
,
2
]
, with which we can prepare various desirable energy eigenstates.

Note added

After the appearance of this manuscript, a preprint whose author is one of us
[
43
]
pointed out that the analysis of the time-independent MPF relying on the convergence of BCH formula
[
1
]
is insufficient for the size-efficient cost reflecting the commutator scaling.
The analysis of the time-independent MPFs in the first version of our preprint had the same problem.
We modify the proofs in Appendix
C
while the resulting cost does not essentially change.

Appendix A
Properties of smooth Hamiltonians

A.1
Extrapolation of smooth Hamiltonians

As discussed in Section
III.1
, our results apply to generic smooth Hamiltonians.
This is based on the fact that there exists a time-periodic extrapolation for such a smooth Hamiltonian, and here we explicitly construct it.
We first begin with defining the smoothness of time-dependency.

Definition A1
.

(Smoothness)

A time-dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)
is said to be of class
C^{p}
in the closed interval
[0,t]
if it has a
q
-th derivative
H^{(q)}(t)
, which is bounded and continuous in
[0,t]
, for every
q=0,1,\cdots,p
.
Note that the derivatives at the edges of
[0,t]
are defined by the left and the right derivatives respectively.

We consider the
p
-th order PF for a smooth Hamiltonian
H(t)=\sum_{\gamma}H_{\gamma}(t)
.
While
H_{\gamma}(t)
is not necessarily time-periodic, it is known that its time-periodic extrapolation
H^{\mathrm{ex}}(t)
can be organized by using so-called a bump function.

Theorem A2
.

(Extrapolation)

When
H_{\gamma}(t)
is a Hamiltonian of class
C^{p+2}
in the interval
[t_{0},t+t_{0}]
, there exists a Hamiltonian
H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(t)
such that the following conditions,

(i)

H^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau)
is a class-
C^{p+2}
Hamiltonian in
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, satisfying the periodicity
H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(\tau+T)=H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(\tau)
for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
with the
\gamma
-independent period
T=2t
,

(ii)

H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(\tau)=H_{\gamma}(\tau)
for any
\tau\in[t_{0},t+t_{0}]
,

(iii)

The Fourier series for
H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(t)
and its derivatives shows the uniform and absolute convergence in
[t_{0},t+t_{0}]
with

\qquad\derivative[p^\prime]{t}H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(t)=\lim_{M\to\infty}\sum_{|m|\leq M}(-im\omega)^{p^{\prime}}H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma m}e^{-im\omega t},

(185)

are satisfied.

Proof.—
We set
t_{0}=0
without loss of generality.
Using a bump function given by

b(t)=\begin{cases}e^{-1/t}&(t>0)\\
0&(t\leq 0),\end{cases}

(186)

we define a scalar function
c(t)
by

c(t)=\frac{\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau b(\tau)b(1-\tau)}{\int_{0}^{1}\differential\tau b(\tau)b(1-\tau)}.

(187)

The function
c(t)
is of class
C^{\infty}
in
t\in\mathbb{R}
and satisfies
c(t)=0
for any
t\leq 0
,
c(t)=1
for any
t\geq 1
, and
c^{(p^{\prime})}(0)=c^{(p^{\prime})}(1)=0
for any
p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.
The extrapolation
H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(t)
is explicitly organized by

H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(\tau)=\begin{cases}H_{\gamma}(\tau)&(\tau\in[0,t])\\
\sum_{k=0}^{p+2}\frac{H^{(k)}_{\gamma}(t)\tau^{k}}{k!}c\left(7-\frac{6\tau}{t}\right)&(\tau\in(t,\frac{4}{3}t))\\
0&(\tau\in[\frac{4}{3}t,\frac{5}{3}t])\\
\sum_{k=0}^{p+2}\frac{H^{(k)}_{\gamma}(0)\tau^{k}}{k!}c\left(\frac{6\tau}{t}-11\right)&(\tau\in(\frac{5}{3}t,2t)).\end{cases}

(188)

The period
T=2t
is independent of
\gamma
, and
H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(\tau)
for
\tau\notin[0,2t]
is by the periodic extension
H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(\tau+2t)=H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(\tau)
.
Confirming the continuity at
\tau=0,t,\frac{4}{3}t,\frac{5}{3}t,2t
, it is easy to check the satisfaction of (i) and (ii).

About the condition (iii), the Fourier coefficient of
H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma}(t)
is

H^{\mathrm{ex}}_{\gamma m}=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\differential\tau H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{ex}}(\tau)e^{im\omega\tau}.

(189)

Repeating the integration by part, we obtain

\displaystyle\norm{H^\mr{ex}_{\gamma m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\norm{\frac{i^{p+2}}{(m\omega)^{p+2}T} \int_0^T \dd\tau H^{\mr{ex} \, (p+2)}_\gamma(\tau) e^{im\omega\tau}}

(190)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(|m|\omega)^{p+2}}\max_{\tau\in[0,2t]}\left(\norm{H^\mr{ex}_\gamma(\tau)}\right)

for each
m\neq 0
, and it decays as
\norm{H^\mr{ex}_{\gamma m}}\in o(|m|^{-p-1})
.
This implies the uniform and absolute convergence of Eq. (
185
), which validates the condition (iii).
\quad\square

When
H(t)
is a smooth Hamiltonian such that each
H_{\gamma}(t)
is of class
C^{\infty}
, the Fourier coefficient
H_{\gamma m}^{\mathrm{ex}}
decays faster than every polynomial of
m
.

A.2
Decay in the Floquet-Hilbert space

We here discuss the decay of operators on the Floquet-Hilbert space, which will be used for proving the error bounds.
It is characterized by the smoothness, inheriting the decaying property of the Fourier components.
First, we show the following lemma. which allows us to easily evaluate the decay in the product of operators.

Lemma A3
.

When two bounded operators
A^{F},B^{F}
acting on the Floquet-Hilbert space satisfy

\norm{\braket{l|A^F|l'}}\in o\left(|l-l^{\prime}|^{-p-1}\right),\quad\norm{\braket{l|B^F|l'}}\in o\left(|l-l^{\prime}|^{-p-1}\right),

(191)

the following inequality is satisfied,

\norm{\braket{l|A^FB^F|l'}}\in o\left(|l-l^{\prime}|^{-p-1}\right).

(192)

Proof.—

Without loss of generality, we suppose

\norm{\braket{l|A^F|l'}}\leq\frac{a}{(1+|l-l^{\prime}|)^{p+1}},\quad\norm{\braket{l|B^F|l'}}\leq\frac{b}{(1+|l-l^{\prime}|)^{p+1}},

(193)

with some constants
a,b\geq 0
.
Inserting the completeness
\sum_{l^{\prime\prime}}\ket{l^{\prime\prime}}\bra{l^{\prime\prime}}=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\norm{\braket{l|A^FB^F|l'}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\braket{l|A^F|l''}}\times\norm{\braket{l''|B^F|l'}}

(194)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{a}{(1+|l-l^{\prime\prime}|)^{p+1}}\times\frac{b}{(1+|l^{\prime\prime}-l^{\prime}|)^{p+1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{p}ab}{(1+|l-l^{\prime}|)^{p+1}},

where
C_{p}
denotes a positive constant dependent on
p
.
The last inequality can be confirmed by an elementary calculation
[
18
]
.
This completes the proof of Eq. (
192
).
\quad\square

Combining the decay of the Fourier components by the smoothness and this lemma, we obtain the decay of the time-evolution operator in the Floquet-Hilbert space as follows.

Lemma A4
.

When the Hamiltonian
H(t)=\sum_{m}H_{m}e^{-im\omega t}
has polynomially-decaying Fourier coefficients as
\norm{H_m}\in o(|m|^{-p-1})
, we have

\norm{ \braket{l|e^{-iH^\mr{Add}t}|l'}}\in o(|l-l^{\prime}|^{-p-1}),\quad\norm{ \braket{l|e^{-iH^Ft}|l'}}\in o(|l-l^{\prime}|^{-p-1}),

(195)

under
|l-l^{\prime}|\to\infty
, where
H^{\mathrm{Add}}
and
H^{F}
are respectively defined by Eqs. (
31
) and (
30
).

Proof.—

Since
H^{\mathrm{Add}}=\sum_{m}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes H_{m}
is a Toeplitz matrix
[
19
]
, its norm is bounded by

\norm{H^\mr{Add}}\leq\max_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left(\norm{H(\tau)}\right).

(196)

This allows us to employ the Taylor series expansion for
e^{-iH^{\mathrm{Add}}t}
.
The matrix element
\braket{l|e^{-iH^{\mathrm{Add}}t}|l^{\prime}}
is bounded by

\displaystyle\norm{ \braket{l|e^{-iH^\mr{Add}t}|l'} }

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{|t|^{n}}{n!}\norm{\braket{l|(H^\mr{Add})^n|l'}}.

(197)

We have
\norm{\braket{l|H^\mr{Add}|l'}}=\norm{H_{l-l'}}\in o(|l-l^{\prime}|^{-p-1})
.
By repeating Lemma
A3
, there exists a constant
C_{p}
such that

\norm{\braket{l|(H^\mr{Add})^n|l'}}\leq\frac{[C_{p}\max_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left(\norm{H(\tau)}\right)]^{n}}{(1+|l-l^{\prime}|)^{p+1}}.

(198)

As a result, we obtain

\norm{ \braket{l|e^{-iH^\mr{Add}t}|l'} }\leq\frac{e^{C_{p}t\max_{\tau\in[0,T]}\left(\norm{H(\tau)}\right)}}{(1+|l-l^{\prime}|)^{p+1}}\in o\left(|l-l^{\prime}|^{-p-1}\right)

(199)

for any finite time
t
, which proves the first half of Eq. (
195
).

For the matrix element
\braket{l|e^{-iH^{F}t}|l^{\prime}}
, we should be careful of the unboundedness of
H^{F}
.
Using the interaction picture in
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
, it is expressed by

\displaystyle\braket{l|e^{-iH^{F}t}|l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bra{l}e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}t}\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-i\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau e^{-iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau}H^{\mathrm{Add}}e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau}\right)\ket{l^{\prime}}

(200)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{il\omega t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau_{n}\cdots\int_{0}^{\tau_{2}}\differential\tau_{1}\bra{l}\prod_{n^{\prime}=1}^{n}e^{-iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau_{n^{\prime}}}H^{\mathrm{Add}}e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau_{n^{\prime}}}\ket{l^{\prime}}.

The second line comes from the boundedness of
e^{-iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau}H^{\mathrm{Add}}e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau}
.
We can apply Lemma
A3
owing to the relation
\|\braket{l|e^{-iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau}H^{\mathrm{Add}}e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\tau}|l^{\prime}}\|=\|H_{l-l^{\prime}}e^{-i(l-l^{\prime})\omega\tau}\|=\|H_{l-l^{\prime}}\|
, and this immediately leads to the latter part of Eq. (
195
).
\quad\square

Appendix B
Proof of the error bounds not mentioned in the main text

B.1
Proof of the generalized PF error bound

In this section, we describe the proof of Theorem
12
, which states the upper bound on the generalized PF error,
\norm{U(t,0)-S(t,0)}
.

Proof of Theorem
12
.—

The proof is completely parallel to that for the standard PF in Section
V
.
The time-independent PF in the Floquet-Hilbert space,
T^{F}(t)
, by Eq. (
) is expressed by

T^{F}(t)\equiv\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}t},

(201)

where
\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
are respectively given by

(\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}})=\begin{cases}(H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{F},\alpha_{v\gamma})&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
(-H^{\mathrm{LP}},\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma}-\alpha_{v\gamma})&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma)\end{cases}

(202)

instead of Eq. (
74
).
The coefficient
\tilde{\alpha}_{v\gamma}
has an absolute value smaller than
1
owing to Eqs. (
25
)-(
26
).
In a similar manner to Eq. (
89
), we obtain

\norm{U(t,0)-S(t,0)}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\sum_{l \in\bbZ} \braket{l|\Delta^F(\tau)|0}},

(203)

where the operator
\Delta^{F}(\tau)
is equal to Eq. (
77
) with substituting Eq. (
202
) as
(\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}})
.

We next examine how the operators
C_{1}(H^{F})
and
C_{2}(\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}})
in
\Delta^{F}(\tau)
form the nested commutators and the time derivative.
As a counterpart of Lemma
9
, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma B1
.

Let
\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
be an operator defined by Eq. (
202
).
The commutators with
\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes(D_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(t)H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t))_{m},\quad D_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(t)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(t)}+i\derivative{t}&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
\displaystyle i\derivative{t}&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma)\end{cases},

(204)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H^{\mathrm{LP}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(i\derivative{t}H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(t)\right)_{m}&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
\displaystyle 0&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma)\end{cases},

(205)

for
\gamma=1,\cdots,\Gamma
.

Proof.—

It is easy to check this lemma.
For an odd integer
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}},H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}]-[H^{\mathrm{LP}},H_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{Add}}]

(206)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Add}_{m}\otimes\left(\mathrm{ad}_{H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}}H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)+i\derivative{t}H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(t)H_{\gamma^{\prime}}(t)\right)_{m},

in which the second line comes from Lemma
9
.
The same goes also for the other cases.
\quad\square

We also need to show the counterpart of Lemma
10
.
With using the truncated generalized PF
S_{\prec v\gamma}(t,0)=\prod_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\prec v\gamma}U_{\pi_{v^{\prime}}(\gamma^{\prime})}(\beta_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}t+\alpha_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}t,\beta_{v^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}}t)
like Eq. (
103
), we have

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-il\omega\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})}\braket{l|e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tau_{1}}T^{F}_{\prec v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau)|0}=\begin{cases}U_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}(\beta_{v\gamma}\tau+\alpha_{v\gamma}\tau_{1},\beta_{v\gamma}\tau)S_{\prec v\gamma}(\tau,0)&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1)\\
S_{\prec v\gamma}(\tau,0)&(\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma)\end{cases},

(207)

where the time
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})
is equal to
\beta_{v\gamma}\tau+\alpha_{v\gamma}\tau_{1}
for
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1
and
\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}\tau_{1}+(\beta_{v\gamma}+\alpha_{v\gamma})(\tau-\tau_{1})
for
\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma
.
The time
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})
belongs to
[0,\tau]
for any
\tau_{1}\in[0,\tau]
.
This relation can be confirmed easily like Lemma
10
with using Eq. (
48
).
Since Eq. (
207
) gives a unitary operator, it is irrelevant to the error bound.
As a result, the quantity characterizing the generalized PF error is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\norm{\sum_{l \in\bbZ} \braket{l|\Delta^F(\tau)|0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tau^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{(V_{p},2\Gamma)}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{(V_{p},2\Gamma)}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}=1}^{\Gamma}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p 2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\gamma'}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))}+\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},V_p 2\Gamma}^{\{q\},\mr{LP}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))}\right)

(208)

\displaystyle\qquad+\tau^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}:\text{odd}}\sum_{v\tilde{\gamma}\preceq v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v'\tilde{\gamma}'}^{\{q\},\frac{\tilde{\gamma}'+1}2}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\tau^{p-1}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\sum_{v^{\prime}=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}=1}^{2\Gamma}\sum_{v\tilde{\gamma}\preceq v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\cdots,q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq 0:\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}+\cdots+q_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}=p,\\
q_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\norm{C_{v\tilde{\gamma},v'\tilde{\gamma}'}^{\{q\},\mr{LP}}(\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}} (\tau,\tau_1))},

in which we properly replace the operator
\bar{D}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(t)
, the coefficient
\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}
, and the time
\tau_{v\tilde{\gamma}}(\tau,\tau_{1})
by those for the generalized PF.
We note that the range of the index
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
in the third line is different from the original one, Eq. (
113
).
This is because
\tilde{H}_{v^{\prime}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}
appearing in the second term of Eq. (
77
) contains
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
also for odd
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
according to Eq. (
202
).
Finally, repeating the evaluation from Eq. (
113
) to Eq. (
121
), we obtain the error bound of the generalized PF,

\norm{U(t,0)-S(t,0)}\leq 5(V_{p}t)^{p+1}\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau),

(209)

where the quantity
\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
is defined by Eq. (
46
).
The change in the coefficient from Eq. (
112
) reflects that in the range of
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
discussed above.
\quad\square

B.2
Error bounds of the time-dependent PFs with
\beta_{11}\neq 0

In the main text, we focus on the standard or generalized PFs with setting
\beta_{11}=0
in Eqs. (
16
) or (
22
).
This is just for simplifying the calculation in Sections
IV
and
V
, and hence we briefly discuss the case with
\beta_{11}\neq 0
here.

We focus on the standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
with
\beta_{11}\neq 0
.
The problem of the calculation in Section
V
is that the time-independent PF
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
defined by Eq. (
49
) cannot approximate the time evolution
e^{-iH^{F}t}
in the case of
\beta_{11}\neq 0
.
Namely, the corresponding PF
\bar{T}(t)
by Eq. (
72
) cannot satisfy the order condition
\bar{T}(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
for a Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}
, which is required in Theorem
11
.
To see this, let us consider the total evolution time under
H_{\gamma}
included in the PF
\bar{T}(t)
.
The evolution time for each
\gamma\in\{1,2,\cdots,\Gamma\}
amounts to

\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\gamma^{\prime}:\pi_{v}(\gamma^{\prime})=\gamma}\alpha_{v\gamma^{\prime}}t=t,

(210)

which directly follows from the assumption, Eq. (
25
).
On the other hand, the total evolution time for
\gamma=0
is

\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t=\beta_{V_{p}(\Gamma+1)}t-\beta_{11}t=t-\beta_{11}t,

(211)

which cannot be equal to
t
except for
\beta_{11}=0
.
Thus, the PF
\bar{T}(t)
cannot approximate
e^{-iHt}=e^{-i\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}t}
even within an error
\order{t}
under
\beta_{11}\neq 0
, implying the impossibility of satisfying the order condition
\bar{T}(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
.
This problem is easily resolved by modifying the time-independent PF
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
on the Floquet-Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{FT}}
.
Instead of Eq. (
49
), we set it by

\bar{T}^{F}(t)=\left[\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right)\right]e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{11}t}.

(212)

The correspondence between the time-independent and -dependent PFs, i.e., the relation
\bar{S}(t,0)=\sum_{l}e^{-il\omega t}\braket{l|\bar{T}(t)|0}
, still holds under this modification due to
e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{11}t}\ket{0}=\ket{0}
.
Then, the corresponding PF
\bar{T}(t)
is Eq. (
72
) multiplied by
e^{iH_{0}\beta_{11}t}
on the right.
The evolution time under
H_{\gamma}
in
\bar{T}(t)
is
t
for every
\gamma=0,1,\cdots,\Gamma
, and hence the satisfaction of the order condition
\bar{T}(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
is allowed.

The error bound of the time-dependent PFs should be modified from Theorem
11
in the case of
\beta_{11}\neq 0
as well.
Section
V
employs the error analysis of the time-dependent PF
[
12
]
for
\bar{T}^{F}(t)
.
It is sufficient to replace the expression Eqs. (
73
) and (
74
) by

\bar{T}^{F}(t)\equiv\prod_{v=0}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\tilde{\gamma}=1}^{2\Gamma}e^{-i\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}t},\quad(\tilde{H}_{v\tilde{\gamma}},\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}})=\begin{cases}(H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}},0)&\text{if $v=0$, $\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1$}\\
(-H^{\mathrm{LP}},\beta_{11}\delta_{\gamma,1})&\text{if $v=0$, $\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma$}\\
(H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}},\alpha_{v\gamma})&\text{if $v\neq 0$, $\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma-1$}\\
(-H^{\mathrm{LP}},\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})&\text{if $v\neq 0$, $\tilde{\gamma}=2\gamma$}\end{cases},

(213)

where
\gamma
is an integer in
\{1,2,\cdots,\Gamma\}
for reproducing the modified PF by Eq. (
212
).
It shares the structure with the original expression by Eqs. (
73
) and (
74
) in that they are organized by the time-evolution operators under
H_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{Add}}
or
H^{\mathrm{LP}}
and that their coefficients satisfy
|\tilde{\alpha}_{v\tilde{\gamma}}|\leq 1
.
On the other hand, the number of the layers
V_{p}
is replaced by
V_{p}+1
.
As a result, we obtain the following error bound valid for the case with
\beta_{11}\neq 0
.

Corollary B2
.

(Standard PF error in the case of
\beta_{11}\neq 0
)

Suppose that the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
are chosen so that a time-independent PF,

\bar{T}(t)=\left[\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{-iH_{0}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right)\right]e^{-iH_{0}\beta_{11}t}

(214)

can satisfy the order condition
\bar{T}(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
for the Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}
.
The standard PF
\bar{S}(t,0)
by Eq. (
16
) has an error bounded by

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)}\leq 4[(V_{p}+1)t]^{p+1}\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau).

(215)

Proof.—

We follow the calculation in the proof of Theorem
11
.
The explicit error bound is immediately obtained by replacing
V_{p}
in Eq. (
112
) with
V_{p}+1
.
\quad\square

We realize that the upper bound Eq. (
215
) excessively overestimates the PF error since Eq. (
213
) contains redundant copies of
H_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{\mathrm{Add}}
for
v=0
.
However, the above error bound is sufficient for ensuring that the scalings of the error bound or the computational cost in the case of
\beta_{11}\neq 0
are exactly the same as those for
\beta_{11}=0
, discussed in the main text.
When the standard PF has uniform coefficients as Definition
1
, the quantity
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau)
in Eq. (
215
) is replaced by
\bar{\alpha}_{\mathrm{com},p}^{\mathrm{uni}}(\tau)
like Corollary
13
.

The same discussion goes also for the generalized PF
S(t,0)
.
In the corresponding time-independent PF for a Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}+(\Gamma-1)H_{0}
by Eq. (
123
), the total evolution time under
H_{0}
amounts to

-\sum_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\sum_{\gamma=1}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma}-\alpha_{v\gamma})t=(\Gamma-1+\beta_{11})t.

(216)

We use the assumption Eq. (
25
) for the above summation of the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma}\}
.
The evolution time does not coincides with
t
unless
\beta_{11}\neq 0
, implying the impossibility of the order condition.
In a similar manner to the standard PF, it is sufficient to employ the modified time-independent PF,

T^{F}(t)=\left[\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma}-\alpha_{v\gamma})t}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}^{F}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right)\right]e^{iH^{\mathrm{LP}}\beta_{11}t}

(217)

instead of Eq. (
), which satisfies the relation, Eq. (
52
).
As a result, we obtain the error bound of the generalized PF
S(t,0)
in the case of
\beta_{11}\neq 0
as follows.

Corollary B3
.

(Generalized PF error in the case of
\beta_{11}\neq 0
)

Suppose that the coefficients
\{\alpha_{v\gamma},\beta_{v\gamma}\}
are chosen so that a time-independent PF,

\bar{T}(t)=\left[\prod_{v=1}^{V_{p}}\prod_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}\left(e^{iH_{0}(\beta_{v(\gamma+1)}-\beta_{v\gamma}t-\alpha_{v\gamma})}e^{-iH_{\pi_{v}(\gamma)}\alpha_{v\gamma}t}\right)\right]e^{iH_{0}\beta_{11}t}

(218)

can satisfy the order condition
T(t)=e^{-iHt}+\order{t^{p+1}}
for the Hamiltonian
H=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\Gamma}H_{\gamma}+(\Gamma-1)H_{0}
.
The generalized PF
S(t,0)
by Eq. (
22
) has an error bounded by

\norm{U(t,0)-\bar{S}(t,0)}\leq 5[(V_{p}+1)t]^{p+1}\max_{\tau\in[0,t]}\alpha_{\mathrm{com},p}(\tau).

(219)

Proof.—
It is sufficient to replace
V_{p}
by
V_{p}+1
in Theorem
12
like the proof of Corollary
B2
.
\quad\square

Appendix C
Proof of time-dependent MPF error

Figure C1:
Proof sketch for an upper bound on the time-dependent MPF error.

In this section, we provide the detailed derivation of the error for the time-dependent multi-product formula (MPF), discussed in Section
VII
.
The time-dependent MPFs are related to the time-independent ones by Theorem
16
.
As a result, their errors are also related to time-independent ones like Eq. (
165
).
Throughout this appendix, we mainly focus on the standard MPF
\bar{N}(t)
by Eq. (
156
).
The derivation of the time-dependent MPF error is sketched in Fig.
C1
, which is parallel to the time-independent case
[
43
]
.
First, we introduce the truncated Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula for approximating the time-independent PF and discuss its properties in Appendix
C.1
.
We next show that it can approximate the time-dependent MPF with an arbitrarily small error in Appendix
C.2
, while some inequalities used in this part are proven in Appendix
C.3
.
Finally, by evaluating the error between the exact time-evolution operator and the approximate one composed of the truncated BCH formulas in Appendix
C.4
, we arrive at the upper bound of the time-dependent MPF error shown as Theorem
17
.

As discussed in the beginning of Section
VII
, the Hamiltonian
H(t)
is composed of the terms
H_{\gamma}(t)
in class
C^{\infty}
.
It is supposed to be
k
-local and have the extensiveness
g
defined by Eq. (
129
).
We also assume the existence of the degree of time-dependency
f
satisfying Eq. (
131
) for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
.

C.1
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula and its properties

In order to evaluate the time-independent MPF error
\bar{M}^{F}(t)-e^{-iH^{F}t}
, we first consider the error
